4
Introduction
This report examines the science results for New Zealand Year 9 students from 
the Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) in 2014/151. 
Along with the reports on New Zealand’s results for science at Year 5, and for 
mathematics at Years 5 and 9, this report forms part of a series of publications 
about New Zealand’s participation in TIMSS 2014/15 (Caygill, Singh & Hanlar, 
2016a,b; Caygill, Hanlar, & Singh, 2016). International findings for science for 
TIMSS 2014/15 have been published by the IEA,2 along with a separate international 
report for mathematics (Martin, Mullis, Foy, & Hooper, 2016; Mullis, Martin, Foy, & 
Hooper, 2016). 
This report examines New Zealand’s science achievement in relation to other 
countries that participated in the study, as well as trends at the Year 9 level from 
1994 to 2014. An examination of the TIMSS assessment questions, in relation 
to New Zealand’s science curriculum, is presented, followed by analyses of 
achievement by sub-groupings (such as gender and ethnicity), and student 
background factors. Comprehensive coverage of background questions about 
teaching and learning, as well as the school context for learning, is also provided.
1	
Internationally this cycle of the study is called TIMSS 2015. As New Zealand conducted TIMSS towards the end of 2014, it is 
referred to as TIMSS 2014/15 throughout this report.
2	
The International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA) is an independent, international cooperative 
of national research institutions and governmental research agencies. It conducts large-scale comparative studies of educational 
achievement and other aspects of education.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

What is TIMSS?
The Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) is a large-scale comparative study of 
mathematics and science achievement at the fourth and eighth grades (Years 5 and 9) around the world. 
As well as examining student achievement, it also monitors curricular implementation, and aims to identify 
the most promising instructional practices from around the world.
Conducted on a regular four-year cycle, TIMSS has assessed mathematics and science in 1994/953, 1998/99, 
2002/03, 2006/07, 2010/11 and 2014/15, with planning underway internationally for 2018/19.
A consortium is responsible for managing the international activities required for the project. This consortium 
consists of: the International Study Center, Lynch School of Education at Boston College, (Massachusetts) 
United States; the IEA Secretariat in Amsterdam, the Netherlands; the IEA’s Data Processing Centre in 
Hamburg, Germany; Statistics Canada in Ottawa, Canada; and the Educational Testing Service (ETS) in 
Princeton, New Jersey in the United States. In New Zealand the Comparative Education Research Unit in the 
Ministry of Education is responsible for carrying out TIMSS.
What value does TIMSS offer?
Although TIMSS is useful for international benchmarking purposes, the real value of TIMSS lies in its ability to 
provide a rich picture of mathematics and science achievement within New Zealand over time.
TIMSS (along with other international assessment studies) can provide information about the performance 
of the New Zealand education system at the national level, within a global context. The information from 
studies such as TIMSS is used in the development and review of policy frameworks, and also to inform and 
improve teaching practice. Developments arising out of previous cycles of TIMSS include resource materials 
for schools and teachers, along with teacher in-service training programmes.
What does TIMSS consist of?
TIMSS consists of assessments of students’ achievements in mathematics and science, along with 
questionnaires for students, their teachers, and principals to gather background information. The background 
information provides a context within which the achievement can be examined.
The TIMSS assessments were developed cooperatively with representatives from participating countries. 
Questions were field-tested with a representative sample of students in participating countries, and 
the results generated were used to select and refine the questions for the final test. Questions for the 
background questionnaires underwent a similar process.
Assessment framework for science in TIMSS
As part of the TIMSS methodology, the international consortium develops an assessment framework, 
which guides the development of the test items and the context questionnaires (Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013). 
The TIMSS assessments are organised around two dimensions: a content dimension specifying the domains 
or subject matter to be assessed within mathematics and science; and a cognitive dimension specifying the 
domains or thinking processes to be assessed. 
These domains differ for middle primary and lower secondary students to reflect the science curriculum 
taught at each level. The four content dimensions in the framework for science at the lower secondary level 
(Year 9 level in New Zealand) are:
3	
Note that this cycle of the study is called TIMSS 1995 internationally, as most countries participated in 1995. However, southern 
hemisphere countries conducted the assessment towards the end of 1994, so in New Zealand reports the study is referred to as 
TIMSS 1994/95. Similarly for the subsequent cycles, the two years in which administration occurred in participating countries are 
indicated.

biology – the characteristics and life processes of organisms; cells and their functions; life cycles, 
reproduction, and heredity; diversity, adaptation, and natural selection; ecosystems; and human health;
»»
chemistry – the composition of matter; properties of matter; and chemical change;
»»
physics – physical states and changes in matter; energy transformation and transfer; light and sound; 
electricity and magnetism; and forces and motion;
»»
Earth science – Earth’s structure and physical features; Earth’s processes, cycles, and history; Earth’s 
resources, their use and conservation; and Earth’s place in the solar system and the universe.
In order to answer questions in the TIMSS test correctly, as well as being familiar with the science content, 
students need to draw on a range of cognitive skills. This is acknowledged in the framework under the 
cognitive domain as:
»»
knowing – covers students’ knowledge of facts, relationships, processes, concepts, and equipment;
»»
applying – focuses on the ability of students to apply knowledge of facts, relationships, processes, 
concepts, equipment and methods;
»»
reasoning – requires students to engage in reasoning to analyse data and other information, draw 
conclusions, and extend their understandings to new situations. 
Exhibit 1 below shows how the TIMSS framework matches to the New Zealand Curriculum and the content 
and cognitive demands that are expected at the lower secondary level (Grade 8 or Year 9). Refer to Chapter 
3 for more information on the framework, especially around the content and cognitive domains.

Who participated?
In TIMSS 2014/15, approximately 270,000 students, 31,000 teachers, and 8,000 schools from 39 countries 
(three of which tested students at a higher grade) and 7 economies (known as benchmarking participants) 
took part at the lower secondary level4. A list of countries and benchmarking participants at the lower 
secondary level can be found in Exhibit 2. Benchmarking participants will not be referred to in this report.
In this cycle of TIMSS, both Year 5 and Year 9 students from New Zealand participated. Schools in New Zealand 
were sampled so that there was no overlap between the samples. In New Zealand, there were 8,101 students 
and 865 teachers from 137 schools assessed at the Year 9 level in October and November 2014.

How was TIMSS administered?
Each lower secondary student was assessed in two timed sessions of 45 minutes, and answered a 
combination of mathematics and science questions. The assessment was a pencil-and-paper test 
containing both multiple-choice and constructed-response questions. Following this, students were given 
a questionnaire containing questions about themselves, their opinions about mathematics and science, 
their computer use, their attitudes to school and leisure activities. Principals and teachers were also given 
questionnaires in order to gain further information about the context in which science teaching and learning 
takes place. In New Zealand, the assessments and questionnaires were conducted in English5.
4	
Some countries only tested students who were much older than the target population. For example, lower secondary students should be 
around 14 years old, according to the design of TIMSS (in the eighth grade or the year level where the average age is closest to 14). 
However, in some countries these children have not covered enough of the material to achieve adequately on the TIMSS tests, so the 
country has decided to test much older children. Throughout this report, the countries that tested at a higher grade and the benchmarking 
participants are not discussed and do not appear in any totals or comparisons.
5	
 In 2002/03, tests and questionnaires were also translated into te reo Māori, but in order to make comparisons between each of 
the cycles, these students were excluded from analyses presented in this report.

What procedures were used to ensure the quality of the 
data?
Members of the consortium ensured procedures were adhered to by all participating countries. TIMSS 
procedures are designed to ensure the reliability, validity, and comparability of the data through:
»»
careful planning and documentation;
»»
cooperation among participating countries;
»»
standardised procedures;
»»
attention to quality control throughout. 
Procedures included:
»»
verification of translations and layout of booklets and questionnaires;
»»
monitoring of sampling activities;
»»
international and national quality control observers during test administration;
»»
checking of data;
»»
detailed manuals covering procedures;
»»
rigorous training for all involved.
Further information
A lot of information is gathered during the TIMSS administration, and a number of techniques are applied 
when collecting and analysing the data. The Methods and procedures in TIMSS 2015 report (Martin, Mullis, & 
Hooper (Eds.), 2016) contains a detailed account of the assessment framework and instrument development, 
sampling, translation of materials, survey operations, quality assurance, creating the international databases, 
and scaling the achievement data. In addition, when the database is made available, it will be accompanied 
by a guide on how to conduct analysis. Brief details of the technical information are given in the definitions 
and technical notes at the end of this report.
In order to provide a context in which the TIMSS results can be examined, TIMSS also publishes the TIMSS 
2015 encyclopedia: Education policy and curriculum in mathematics and science (Mullis, Martin, Goh, & Cotter 
(Eds.), 2016). This encyclopaedia contains short reports from each country describing mathematics and 
science education policies and practices in that country.

Science achievement 
of New Zealand Year 9 
students since 1994 
– 20 years of trends
CHAPTER 1:
 Key Findings
The average (mean) science achievement of New Zealand Year 9 students in 2014/15 was the 
same as that of the students 20 years ago, and was also the same as in 2010/11.
The gap between the high and low achievers (range) for Year 9 students in 2014/15 was much 
wider than in 2002/03; the change has occurred at both ends of the spectrum, with the top 
higher than in 2002/03, and the bottom lower than in 2002/03.
New Zealand Year 9 students’ mean science achievement was significantly higher than 20 
countries, but lower than the mean score of 14 countries.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

Back to Contents

According to the New Zealand Curriculum, science is a way of investigating, 
understanding, and explaining our natural, physical world, and the wider universe 
(Ministry of Education, 2007). “Improving New Zealand’s economic, social and 
environmental outcomes through growing an innovative society drives the need for 
an increasingly science, technology, engineering and mathematics (STEM)-competent 
workforce” (Ministry of Business, Innovation and Employment and Ministry of Education, 
2014). The national strategic plan for science in society, A Nation of Curious Minds, was 
launched in July 2014, in response to the Government’s wish to “encourage and enable 
better engagement with science and technology across all sectors of New Zealand 
society” (Ministry of Business, Innovation and Employment, 2014). 
In 2014, New Zealand participated in the sixth cycle of TIMSS, a large-scale assessment 
of the mathematics and science skills and knowledge of middle primary and lower 
secondary students. This sixth cycle of TIMSS occurred 20 years after the first TIMSS 
assessment, so we now have information about their achievement over this period. 
Thirty-nine countries, including New Zealand, participated at the lower secondary level 
in this cycle, conducted during 2014 and 2015. This chapter will examine the science 
achievement of New Zealand’s Year 9 students, in relation to that of other participating 
countries, and over time.
Science achievement in TIMSS 2014/15
New Zealand’s science achievement in TIMSS can be examined as a snapshot in time compared to other 
countries, but also over time. This first section begins with New Zealand’s achievement in the 2014/15 cycle of 
TIMSS. For reporting, the achievement of all participating students has been put on a scale with a centre point of 
500. This point of 500 was set using the student performance in the 1994/95 cycle of TIMSS, and achievement 
in subsequent cycles was put on the same scale. Achievement was then reported relative to this scale using the 
term scale score points (see Definitions and technical notes for further details on the scales and the centre point).
The average (mean) science score for New Zealand Year 9 students in 2014/15 was 513 scale score points. 
New Zealand’s score was significantly higher than the TIMSS scale centre point, but similar to Lithuania (519), 
Australia (512), Norway (509), and Israel (507), and higher than 20 countries. However, New Zealand’s mean 
score was lower than the mean score of 14 countries, including England, Ireland, the United States, and Canada. 
The highest achieving countries, Singapore, Japan, and Chinese Taipei, had average science achievement 
among their lower secondary students of over 560 scale score points. Of the countries that tested in English, 
Singapore had the highest mean score (597). The next highest mean scores among the countries testing in 
English were England (537), Ireland (530), the United States (530), and Canada (526).
It is also useful to look at the range of achievement – the gap between the high and low achievers. The lowest 
outer limit of the bars presented in Exhibit 1.1 is called the 5th percentile, the score at which only five percent 
of students achieved a lower score. The upper-most limit presented is the 95th percentile, the score at which 
only five percent of students achieved a higher score. The range of achievement from the 5th percentile (357) 
to the 95th percentile (652) for New Zealand Year 9 students was 295 scale score points. New Zealand’s 
range of achievement was wider than the high-performing countries, and most of the predominantly English-
speaking countries. 
The range from the 5th to the 95th percentile can move around quite a lot from cycle to cycle. Therefore, it is 
useful to also look at the inter-quartile range, the difference between the 25th and 75th percentile, for a more 
stable version of the range. The inter-quartile range for New Zealand Year 9 students (123) was still higher 
than all of the high-performing, and most of the English-speaking, countries.

Exhibit 1.2 provides information to help put science achievement in context. It shows selected structural 
characteristics of education, including expenditure on education, to help the reader understand similarities 
and differences between New Zealand and other participating countries. Countries are presented in the same 
order as Exhibit 1.1 for ease of comparison. The GNI (purchasing power parity) is a measure of income for a 
country, converted to U.S. dollars, and adjusted to allow comparison of real levels of expenditure between 
countries (see http://databank.worldbank.org for details).
Many of the high-achieving countries had much higher income per capita than New Zealand, especially when 
purchasing power was taken into account. The exception was the Republic of Korea, whose GNI was a bit 
smaller than New Zealand, and a lot smaller than the other high-achieving countries. In terms of the countries 
that tested in English, all but Malta had higher income per capita than New Zealand.
Trends in New Zealand’s achievement over 5 cycles of 
TIMSS
Mean science achievement in 2014 was not statistically different from any of the previous years, as shown in 
Exhibit 1.3. Although the difference between 2002 and 2014 was seven scale score points, this difference was 
not significant.

Trends on the science test questions from 2010/11 to 2014/15
At the end of each cycle of TIMSS, test questions are released into the public domain. At the beginning of 
the next cycle, new questions are developed to replace released questions. In addition, each cycle of TIMSS 
includes some questions from previous cycles, to provide a trend measure over time. This section presents an 
analysis of the trend questions included in both TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15.
There were 125 questions common to both 2010/11 and 2014/15. Of these 125 questions, 36 questions had 
similar proportions of New Zealand Year 9 students correctly answering them across the two cycles (as 
shown in Exhibit 1.5). Forty percent of the questions (50) showed an increase; that is they were correctly 
answered by more Year 9 students in 2014/15, compared with 2010/11. In contrast, just under one-third of the 
questions (39) showed a decrease; that is they were correctly answered by fewer Year 9 students in 2014/15, 
compared with 2010/11.
In addition to no overall change in the mean science achievement of Year 9 students between 2010/11 and 
2014/15, these item statistics demonstrate this lack of change.

International trends in science achievement at the lower 
secondary level
New Zealand has now participated in five cycles of TIMSS at the lower secondary level; 1994/95, 1998/99, 
2002/03, 2010/11, and 2014/15 (although offered in 2006/07, New Zealand didn’t participate). The design of 
TIMSS allows us to measure trends over time. Exhibit 1.6 presents changes in mean science achievement for 
those countries that have participated in selected cycles of TIMSS. Lithuania is the country with the largest 
increase in science achievement since the 1994/95 cycle, although most of this change happened prior to the 
2002/03 cycle. Slovenia, Hong Kong SAR, and the Russian Federation have also had increases of more than 
20 score points in science achievement since 1994/95. New Zealand, along with England, Australia, and the 
Islamic Republic of Iran have had no significant change since 1994/95.

Relative rankings among countries
Many commentators on the international studies focus on New Zealand’s ranking relative to other 
countries. In order to inform this commentary, Exhibit 1.7 presents New Zealand’s relative ranking in science

and 2014/15. Of all the 39 countries that participated in TIMSS 2014/15 at the lower secondary level, only 15

calculate whether apparent differences are real. For example, the score of 511 in New Zealand (1994/95) is not 
significantly different from the score of 514 in Australia (see section entitled Definitions and technical notes 
for details of how significance can be calculated).
Exhibit 1.7 shows that the mean science achievement in New Zealand has been below the mean for the 15 
trend countries in each cycle. In addition, although the mean for all 15 countries has increased, the ranking of 
New Zealand among these 15 countries hasn’t changed between 1994/95 and 2014/15.

Classifying New Zealand 
Year 9 students as low 
to advanced performers 
CHAPTER 2:
 Key Findings
Ten percent of New Zealand Year 9 students were classified as advanced performers (reached 
the advanced benchmark), while 12 percent of Year 9 students were classified as below low 
performers, who did not perform simple science tasks. 
Compared with other countries, New Zealand was higher than the international median 
when considering the proportion of advanced performers, as well as the proportion of lower 
performers. However, high-performing countries had at least one fifth of their lower secondary 
students classified as advanced performers, and four percent or fewer classified as below low 
performers.
There were more Year 9 students who were lower achievers in 2014/15, compared with 
2002/03. The proportion of Year 9 students reaching each benchmark did not change 
significantly between 2010/11 and 2014/15.

In order to describe more fully what achievement on the science scale means, the 
TIMSS international researchers have developed benchmarks. These benchmarks 
link student performance on the TIMSS science scale to performance on science 
questions, and describe what students can typically do at set points on the 
science achievement scale. These benchmarks classify students by the ability they 
demonstrated in the TIMSS assessment. This chapter describes New Zealand Year 
9 students’ science achievement using these benchmarks, compared to other 
countries, and over time.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
19
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»

International science benchmarks
The international benchmarks are four points on the science scale: the advanced benchmark (625 scale 
score points); the high benchmark (550); the intermediate benchmark (475); and the low benchmark (400). 
The performance of students reaching each benchmark is described in relation to the types of questions they 
answered correctly. Exhibit 2.1 presents the 2014/15 descriptions of the international benchmarks of science 
achievement.
Exhibit 2.1: Descriptions of TIMSS 2014/15 international benchmarks of science achievement
Advanced international benchmark — 625
Students communicate understanding of complex concepts related to biology, chemistry, physics and Earth science 
in practical, abstract, and experimental contexts. Students apply knowledge of cells and their functions, as well as characteristics 
and life processes of organisms. They demonstrate understanding of diversity, adaptation, and natural selection among organisms, and 
of ecosystems and the interaction of organisms with their environment. Students apply knowledge of life cycles, and heredity in plants 
and animals. Students demonstrate knowledge of the composition and physical properties of matter, and apply knowledge of chemical 
and physical change in practical and experimental contexts. Students communicate understanding of physical states and changes 
in matter in practical and experimental contexts, apply knowledge of energy transfer, and demonstrate knowledge of electricity and 
magnetism. Students communicate understanding of forces and pressure, and demonstrate knowledge of light and sound in practical 
and abstract situations. Students communicate understanding of Earth’s structure, physical features, and resources, as well as of Earth’s 
place in the solar system. Students show understanding of basic aspects of scientific investigation. They identify which variables to 
control in an experimental situation, compare information from several sources, combine information to predict and draw conclusions, 
and interpret information in diagrams, maps, graphs, and tables to solve problems. They provide written explanations to communicate 
scientific knowledge. 
High international benchmark — 550
Students apply and communicate understanding of concepts from biology, chemistry, physics, and Earth science 
in everyday and abstract situations. Students apply knowledge of cells and their functions, and of the characteristics and life 
processes of organisms. They communicate understanding of ecosystems and the interaction of organisms with their environment, and 
apply some knowledge of human health related to nutrition and infectious disease. Students show some knowledge and understanding 
of the composition and properties of matter and chemical change. They apply basic knowledge of energy transformation and transfer, 
and of light and sound in practical situations, and demonstrate understanding of simple electrical circuits and properties of magnets. 
Students apply their knowledge of forces and motion to everyday and abstract situations. They apply knowledge of Earth’s physical 
features, processes, cycles, and history, and show some understanding of Earth’s resources, their use, and conservation, as well as some 
knowledge of the interaction between the Earth and the Moon. Students demonstrate some scientific inquiry skills, including selecting 
and justifying an appropriate experimental method. They combine and interpret information from various types of diagrams, graphs, 
and tables; select relevant information to analyse and draw conclusions; and provide short explanations conveying scientific knowledge.
Intermediate international benchmark — 475
Students demonstrate and apply their knowledge of biology, chemistry, physics, and Earth science in various 
contexts. Students demonstrate some knowledge of characteristics and life processes of animals and human health. They apply 
knowledge of ecosystems, the interaction of living things, and the adaptation of animals to their environments. Students apply some 
knowledge of the properties of matter. They also show knowledge of some aspects of force, motion, and energy. Students apply 
knowledge of Earth’s processes, resources, and physical features. They interpret information from tables, graphs, and pictorial diagrams 
to draw conclusions, apply knowledge to practical situations, and communicate their understanding through brief descriptive responses.
Low international benchmark — 400
Students show some basic knowledge of biology, chemistry, physics, and Earth science. Students apply basic knowledge 
of ecosystems and adaptation of animals to their environment, show knowledge of basic facts related to thermal and electrical 
conductivity and electromagnetism, and show knowledge of some basic Earth science facts. Students interpret simple pictorial 
diagrams and apply basic knowledge to practical situations.
Source:	 Exhibit 2.8, Martin, Mullis, Foy, & Hooper, 2016.

Exhibit 2.2 presents the benchmarks for all countries in two ways. The graphical part shows the proportions 
of lower secondary students achieving at each of the benchmarks (i.e. above the high benchmark of 
550, but not above the advanced benchmark of 625). The table part shows the proportions of lower 
secondary students achieving at or above each of the benchmarks (i.e. at or above the high benchmark 
of 550, including those above the advanced benchmark). Ten percent of New Zealand Year 9 students 
reached the advanced benchmark, the point where students were deemed capable of communicating their 
understanding of complex concepts related to biology, chemistry, physics and Earth science in practical, 
abstract, and experimental contexts. In comparison, more than 40 percent of the lower secondary students in 
Singapore (42%), and over one-quarter of the lower secondary students in Chinese Taipei (27%) reached this 
advanced level of science ability. There were also fewer advanced lower secondary scientists in New Zealand 
compared with England (14%), but more than Australia (7%) or Canada (7%).
Twelve percent of Year 9 students in New Zealand could not perform many of the simplest tasks TIMSS seeks 
to measure (they correctly completed less than half of the low benchmark tasks). In comparison, only four 
percent or fewer of the lower secondary students in the highest-performing countries performed below this 
low benchmark. There were also fewer really low performing lower secondary students (those who did not 
reach the low benchmark) in Chinese Taipei (4%), England (5%), the United States (7%), and Australia (9%).
Included at the bottom of the exhibit is the international median percentage of students at each benchmark. 
More New Zealand students reached all the benchmarks, compared with the international median.

Changes over time for New Zealand Year 9 students on science 
benchmarks
Since TIMSS began, there has been some concern about the proportion of New Zealand Year 9 students who 
have low achievement in science. We can use the benchmarks to compare how this proportion has changed 
over time. Exhibit 2.3 presents those Year 9 students achieving at each of the benchmarks (as shown in the 
graphical part), and those students achieving at or above each of the benchmarks (as shown in the table 
part) in each cycle from 1994 to 2014.
The proportion of Year 9 students reaching the intermediate and low benchmarks in 2014 was significantly 
lower than in 2002 (i.e. there were more lower achievers in 2014 than in 2002). Although the proportions 
reaching the high and advanced benchmarks look higher, these proportions are not significant. The 
proportion of Year 9 students reaching each benchmark did not change significantly between 2010 and 2014. 
The proportion of Year 9 students not reaching the low benchmark (12% in 2014), at its best in 2002 (6%), 
remains a concern. These students below the low benchmark did not demonstrate basic scientific knowledge.

24
Strengths and weaknesses 
of New Zealand Year 9 
students within science 
CHAPTER 3:
 Key Findings
New Zealand Year 9 students showed greatest strength in biology and Earth science. 
Chemistry remains a relative weakness for New Zealand Year 9 students since 2010/11.
New Zealand Year 9 students did significantly better at applying and reasoning, compared to 
knowing in science.
The New Zealand Year 9 students’ achievements were similar across all science content and 
cognitive domains in both 2010/11 and 2014/15. 
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

To measure trends across time, some of the items from each cycle are kept secure 
and used in the next cycle. The science assessment in 2014/15 had items from the 
previous two cycles, as well as new items. In total there were 220 test questions 
at the lower secondary level in science (Year 9 in New Zealand). As outlined in the 
introductory chapter, the international consortium developed the TIMSS 2014/15 
assessment framework, which guided the development of the test items and the 
context questionnaires (Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013). The assessment framework 
was matched to the curriculum of participating countries to reflect the curricular 
emphasis across countries, and is further explored in Chapter 4. This chapter focuses 
on the strengths and weaknesses of New Zealand Year 9 students in science across 
the two domains that make up the TIMSS 2014/15 assessment framework, the 
content and cognitive domains.
Defining the science content and cognitive domains
The TIMSS science assessment is designed to cover a wide range of the science teaching and learning 
students around the world are likely to undertake. Like the New Zealand Curriculum, the assessment 
framework is arranged by areas of science, and details the content the assessment will cover, termed the 
content domains. In addition to content knowledge, TIMSS also assesses students’ cognitive skills, outlining 
the thinking processes and behaviours required as they engage with the science content (Mullis & Martin 
(Eds.), 2013). 
The content domains for science at the lower secondary level are biology, chemistry, physics and Earth 
science. The cognitive domains are knowing, applying, and reasoning.
Building on the foundations of life science knowledge, the biology domain focuses on students’ 
understanding of many of the most important concepts in biology like characteristics and life processes of 
organisms; cells and their functions; life cycles, reproduction, and heredity; diversity, adaptation, and natural 
selection; ecosystems; and human health. 
The chemistry domain at the Year 9 level focuses on students’ knowledge of the central concepts, such 
as composition and properties of matter; and chemical change; as well as principles that are needed for 
understanding practical applications of chemistry, and undertaking more advanced study in future.
The physics domain focuses on students’ knowledge of the central physics concepts that are needed for 
understanding practical applications of physics, or for undertaking advanced study later in their education. 
At the Year 9 level, the physics domain includes five topic areas: physical states and changes in matter; 
energy transformation and transfer; light and sound; electricity and magnetism; and forces and motion. 
The Earth science dimension focuses on the students’ knowledge about the planet on which they live, and 
its place in the universe. The TIMSS 2015 science framework identifies four topic areas that are universally 
considered to be important in lower secondary students’ understanding of the Earth and its place in the 
universe: Earth’s structure and physical features; Earth’s processes, cycles, and history; Earth’s resources, 
their use and conservation; and Earth in the solar system and the universe. 
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
25
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»

There are three cognitive domains in TIMSS science. The first domain, knowing, assesses the student’s ability 
to recall, recognise, and describe facts, concepts, and procedures that are necessary for a solid foundation 
in science. The second domain, applying, focuses on using this knowledge to generate explanations and 
solve practical problems. The third domain, reasoning, includes using evidence and science understanding to 
analyse, synthesise, and generalise, often in unfamiliar situations and complex contexts. It also includes the 
skills and knowledge that are needed for developing hypotheses and designing scientific investigations.
In 2014/15, TIMSS science also assessed science practices. These practices include skills from daily life and 
school studies that students use in a systematic way to conduct scientific inquiry, and that are fundamental 
to all science disciplines. The TIMSS 2014/15 assessment included five science practices: asking questions 
based on observations; generating evidence; working with data; answering the research question; and 
making an argument from evidence. However, as these practices cannot be assessed in isolation, the 
assessment was done in the context of one of the science content domains, and drew upon the range of 
thinking processes specified in the cognitive domains (Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013).
The distribution of assessment questions varied across the content and cognitive domains. As Exhibit 3.1 
shows, there was more emphasis on biology questions, and the least emphasis on chemistry and Earth 
science questions at the lower secondary level. In accordance with the increased cognitive ability, instruction, 
experience, and breadth and depth of understanding of students at the lower secondary level, there was 
more emphasis on the applying domain than either of the other two cognitive domains.

Science performance in relation to the content and 
cognitive domains
Achievement scores were created for the content and cognitive domains to provide a value so that they 
are comparable with each other. In the early cycles of TIMSS (prior to 2006), a score of 500 on one domain 
was not directly comparable to 500 on another domain. So a new methodology was created and applied to 
questions from the 2006/07 assessment onwards, to ensure that these domains could be compared with each 
other. Therefore, it is important to note that comparisons between domains should only be made between the 
2006/07, 2010/11 and 2014/15 cycles, but not with earlier cycles (Caygill, Kirkham, & Marshall, 2013a). 
As Exhibit 3.2 shows, New Zealand Year 9 students performed relatively better on biology and Earth science 
questions, with similar average scores of 520 and 517 respectively. Chemistry was their weakest domain, 
compared to all other areas, with 498 score points. On the cognitive domains, New Zealand Year 9 students 
did better at tasks that required them to apply their science knowledge and use their reasoning, than those 
that required them to demonstrate their knowledge of science concepts, procedures, and facts (knowing - 
see Exhibit 3.3).

Among the highest performing Asian countries, a significant increase in the mean scale score from the TIMSS

30
CHAPTER 4:
TIMSS and the New Zealand 
science curriculum
 Key Findings
Many New Zealand Year 9 students were working at level 4 of the curriculum by the end 
of the year, rather than level 5. Those students working at higher levels had higher science 
achievement.
When the TIMSS test was compared to curriculum expectations for New Zealand Year 9 
students, there were questions considered more advanced than our curriculum. However, when 
the test was reduced to contain only those appropriate to New Zealand Year 9 students, the 
average student got half the items correct.
New Zealand Year 9 students did best on biology and Earth science questions, but these were 
the areas of the curriculum least likely to be covered by their teachers.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

TIMSS examines all aspects of curriculum: the intended curriculum; the implemented 
curriculum; and the attained curriculum. In New Zealand, the New Zealand 
Curriculum (NZC) lays out what is intended to be implemented and attained, and
this guides teaching and learning. 
In the NZC, the alignment of curriculum levels with year levels is flexible. Teachers 
are expected to tailor lessons to meet students’ individual needs (the implemented 
curriculum). Students in the same year level may be working at different curriculum 
levels, as appropriate to their abilities and pace of progression. 
As in previous cycles of TIMSS, teachers were asked to indicate which topics they 
had taught to their Year 9 students in the current school year (the implemented 
curriculum), and the curriculum level the majority of their Year 9 students were 
working at (the attained curriculum). This section will examine the implementation 
of the curriculum, as well as the match between the TIMSS test and the intended 
curriculum.
Science curriculum levels and the TIMSS content domains
According to the New Zealand Curriculum, by the end of their ninth year of schooling, students will be 
achieving at early level 5 in the science learning area of the curriculum (Ministry of Education, 2007). 
New Zealand teachers were asked at which level(s) of science in the New Zealand Curriculum most of 
their Year 9 students were working for each of the strands: living world; material world; physical world; and 
planet Earth and beyond. Exhibit 4.1 shows that the proportion of Year 9 students working at level 5 of 
the curriculum varied across strands, and that many classes were working across levels of the curriculum. 
Around one in ten Year 9 students were in classes where the majority of students were working at level 
5 of the curriculum on the living world, material world, and physical world strands, with this proportion 
being only seven percent on the planet Earth and beyond strand. Just under 30 percent of Year 9 students 
were working across levels 4 and 5. Results from the National Monitoring Study of Student Achievement 
(Educational Assessment Research Unit & New Zealand Council for Educational Research, 2013) also show 
many students working at lower levels of the curriculum than intended.
We can use the TIMSS content domains to examine attainment on the curriculum strands due to their 
similar science content. For example the physics content domain for lower secondary in TIMSS consists 
of understandings and skills related to five topic areas: physical states and changes in matter; energy 
transformation and transfer; light and sound; electricity and magnetism; and forces and motion (see Jones, 
Wheeler, & Centurino, 2013). Similarly, the physical world strand at level 5 of the NZC consists of: movement; 
forces; electricity and magnetism; light; sound; waves; and heat (Ministry of Education, 2007). 
Exhibit 4.1 shows that Year 9 students whose classes were working at higher levels of the curriculum had 
higher achievement on the associated TIMSS content domain. Examining these results in the international 
context shows that if we had only included those Year 9 students working at level 5 of the curriculum in the 
TIMSS testing, New Zealand would have had a high mean score relative to the high-performing countries, 
but lower than Singapore. For example, the average score for New Zealand Year 9 students working at level 
5 of the living world strand of the curriculum is significantly lower on the corresponding biology content 
domain (576 scale score points) than their Singaporean counterparts (609), but higher than nearly all other 
countries. However, the score for those New Zealand Year 9 students whose classes were working across 
levels 4 and 5 on the biology domain (550) was lower than the high-performing countries.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

Curriculum match
Questions about international studies often focus on the appropriateness of the assessment questions 
for New Zealand students. New Zealand is not unique in asking this question; other countries are also 
concerned with the appropriateness of these tests. The TIMSS assessment questions are developed through 
a collaborative process that begins with the development of an assessment framework. The TIMSS 2015 
science framework (Jones, Wheeler, & Centurino, 2013) was designed to specify the important aspects of 
science that participating countries agreed should be the focus of an international assessment of science 
achievement. However, it is inevitable that the tests included questions that were unfamiliar to some students 
in some countries. In order to investigate the extent to which the TIMSS 2014/15 assessment was relevant to 
each country’s curriculum, TIMSS conducted a Test-Curriculum Matching Analysis (TCMA). The TCMA was 
also used to investigate the impact of selecting only appropriate questions on a country’s performance.
For the TCMA, each assessment question was examined using the following two criteria:
»»
whether or not the topic of the question is in the intended curriculum for the majority (50% or more) of 
lower secondary students in the grade or school level tested (in our case Year 9);
»»
whether or not the item topic was intended to be encountered by the students prior to the TIMSS 
testing (in our case October or November 2014).
All questions, regardless of this analysis, were included in any overall results reported for TIMSS. However, this 
analysis was used to ascertain the level to which the results might change for New Zealand if only questions 
judged appropriate for New Zealand Year 9 students were included in the tests. The analysis also included 
an examination of how students in other countries would fare if they were only given the “New Zealand-
appropriate” test.
Exhibit 4.2 shows the proportion of questions considered appropriate to the New Zealand Curriculum in each 
of the TIMSS content areas. However, it should be noted that New Zealand’s science curriculum provides 
some challenges for deciding whether or not at least half of Year 9 students are likely to have met the 
question topics in the TIMSS test. The curriculum is not prescriptive, instead providing some broad guidelines 
of science concepts and skills that schools can choose to cover. Schools are encouraged to design science 
programmes that are relevant to their students and communities. Consequently, when schools plan their 
science programmes, there is considerable variation between them. Another challenge is that the broad 
achievement objectives are grouped in levels that cover approximately two years of schooling. 
As shown in the previous section, New Zealand Year 9 students were generally working at levels 4 and 5 of 
the curriculum, so information from levels 4 and 5 was used to guide judgements on the TCMA. In addition, 
curriculum-matched resources available on scienceonline.tki.org.nz were used for further clarification (our 
thanks to the teachers who did this work).
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

Coverage of science topics
To ascertain the implemented curriculum, teachers of the selected students were asked what opportunities 
students had to learn the content in the TIMSS tests. Teachers provided information on science topics taught 
to Year 9 students prior to, or during, the year of the TIMSS assessment. For each of 22 topics, teachers 
were asked if the topic was mostly taught before this year, mostly taught this year, or not yet taught or just 
introduced. These 22 topics cover the TIMSS 2015 science framework (Jones, Wheeler, & Centurino, 2013). 
They provide a link between the TIMSS framework and the TIMSS assessment. Note that this question 
is about whether the topic was taught, but does not gather information on the depth or breadth of the 
teaching on the topic. For example, the topic of forces and motion (types of forces, basic description of 
motion, effects of density and pressure) might cover quite a variety of experiences with forces in one 
classroom, but only the very basics in another classroom – this question does not gather that level of detail. 
Half of New Zealand Year 9 students had been taught all these topics in 2014 or the preceding years. 
This proportion was the lowest among the participating countries. In comparison, just under three-quarters 
of lower secondary students (73%), on average, across all participating countries had been taught all of these 
22 topics (ranging from 55% in Hong Kong SAR, to 89% in Jordan – see Exhibit 9.4, Martin, Mullis, Foy, & 
Hooper, 2016).
More New Zealand Year 9 students had been taught chemistry (58%) or physics (55%) topics in 2014, or the 
preceding years, than biology (47%) or Earth science topics (40%). Note that this question was not about 
the proportion of time spent on these, but rather whether the topic was covered or not. As mentioned earlier, 
biology and Earth science were the areas of TIMSS where New Zealand Year 9 students showed the best 
performance, while the other two areas were lower, so this finding is quite surprising.
The science topics covered by the fewest New Zealand Year 9 students were:
»»
the role of electrons in chemical bonds (12%);
»»
electric circuits (flow of current; types of circuits - parallel/series) and properties and uses of permanent 
magnets and electromagnets (13%);
»»
life cycles, sexual reproduction, and heredity (passing on of traits, inherited versus acquired/learned 
characteristics) (17%);
»»
Earth’s processes, cycles, and history (rock cycle; water cycle; weather versus climate; major geological 
events; formation of fossils and fossil fuels) (23%); 
»»
properties and uses of common acids and bases (24%).
Exhibit 4.4 shows a complete list of topics and coverage among New Zealand Year 9 students.
The wording of the topics has changed since the previous cycle of TIMSS in 2010/11, and new items have 
been added. For example, the role of electrons in chemical bonds was not asked about last time. The topic 
electric circuits (flow of current; types of circuits - parallel/series) and properties and uses of permanent 
magnets and electromagnets (14%) had very low proportions of New Zealand Year 9 students exposed to 
the topic last cycle. 
New Zealand Year 9 students who were in classes where the topic was covered (the year of the assessment, 
or in previous years) generally had higher achievement than those where the topic was not covered. 
However, Jordan, Turkey, and Hungary all had more than 85 percent coverage of the topics, but were not 
among the highest performing countries. In contrast, the highest performing countries, Singapore (68%), 
Chinese Taipei (67%), and Japan (60%), all reported lower coverage of the topics.

CHAPTER 5:
Science achievement of 
Year 9 boys and girls
 Key Findings
Year 9 boys and girls had the same science achievement as each other, on average, but boys 
had a wider range than girls.
Year 9 girls had significantly higher science achievement than 20 years ago, while boys’ science 
achievement has not changed much since 1994/95.
Similar proportions of Year 9 girls and boys reached each of the science achievement 
benchmarks in 2014/15. More girls reached the high science benchmark in 2014/15, compared 
to 2010/11, and fewer boys reached the low benchmark (more boys below low). 
In 2014/15, compared to the other gender, girls did better in biology, while boys achieved 
higher on physics and Earth science, with no significant gender difference on chemistry, or the 
cognitive domain areas of knowing, applying and reasoning.
In 2014/15, Year 9 girls saw a significant increase in the biology, applying and reasoning 
domain areas, compared to their counterparts in 2010/11. Year 9 boys, on the other hand, 
saw a significant decrease in the chemistry and knowing domain areas, compared to those in 
TIMSS 2010/11.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

New Zealand has many observed differences in achievement between boys and 
girls, with girls generally outperforming boys (Chamberlain, 2011; New Zealand 
Qualifications Authority, 2011; New Zealand Qualifications Authority, 2014). 
This pattern of girls out-performing boys has also been observed in other countries, 
with the exception of mathematics and science, where in many cases boys achieve 
just as well as girls (Bramley, Vidal Rodeiro, & Vitello, 2015). A Ministry of Education 
synthesis (Ministry of Education, 2007) highlighted several issues around the 
achievement of boys which included:
»	 early problems in reading; 
»	 lower achievement in reading and writing; 
»	 disengagement with school; 
»	 lower qualification attainment.
The synthesis report was supported by a range of qualification and participation 
data, showing different proportions of boys and girls having success. The TIMSS 
1994/95 and 2010/11 studies saw significant differences in science achievement 
between New Zealand Year 9 boys and girls, in favour of boys. This chapter will 
examine the science achievement of Year 9 boys and girls in TIMSS in 2014/15, 
with some comparisons with previous cycles.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

Science achievement of boys and girls
As Exhibit 5.1 shows, the mean science achievement of Year 9 girls increased (though not significantly) 
between 1994/95 and 2002/03. It then significantly dropped between 2002/03 and 2010/11, and significantly 
increased again between 2010/11 and 2014/15 – back to the 2002/03 level. Year 9 boys, on the other hand, 
have had no significant difference in achievement since 1994/95.

As shown in Exhibit 5.2, Year 9 girls and boys had a similar mean science achievement (513 and 512 scale 
score points respectively) in TIMSS 2014/15. The range of achievement for boys, however, was wider than for 
girls, as it has been in all previous TIMSS cycles. In both 1994/95 and 2010/11, Year 9 boys achieved higher, on 
average, than Year 9 girls; for all other cycles there was no significant difference between boys and girls. 
Both Year 9 girls and boys have had similar trends in the range of achievement since 1994/95. The range was 
narrowest for both boys and girls in 2002/03. Since then, the range of achievement for both genders has 
been widening consistently. While the TIMSS 2002/03 results remain the narrowest range for both genders, 
the TIMSS 2014/15 range has been the widest for boys, compared to all previous cycles. The girls’ widest 
range was in 1998/99. For girls, the change between TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15 was primarily at the top of 
the range, but for boys it was at both ends of the spectrum. 
The high-performing countries had no gender difference in achievement, with the exception of Hong Kong 
SAR, which had a significant difference in favour of boys.

Achievement on the content and cognitive domains for boys 
and girls
Although there were no overall differences in mean science achievement between New Zealand Year 9 boys 
and girls in TIMSS 2014/15, girls had higher achievement in biology, while boys achieved higher on physics 
and Earth science. There was no significant difference in the chemistry domain between Year 9 boys and girls 
in 2014/15 (see Exhibit 5.5).
In 2014/15, girls saw a significant increase in the biology, applying and reasoning domain areas, compared 
to their counterparts in 2010/11. Boys, on the other hand, saw a significant decrease in the chemistry and 
knowing domain areas, compared to those in TIMSS 2010/11. Achievement on the rest of the content and 
cognitive domain areas remained similar for both genders between the two cycles.
Internationally, lower secondary girls achieved higher, on average, in biology and chemistry, which was a 
similar pattern in all countries, except those where girls and boys achieved similar scores. On physics and 
Earth science, however, lower secondary boys were either similar or ahead of girls internationally. All high-
achieving countries had boys score higher than girls, except those where girls and boys achieved similar 
scores. Unlike the international pattern, almost all Arab-speaking countries had girls ahead of boys in both 
physics and Earth science.
On the knowing, applying and reasoning cognitive domains, lower secondary girls were ahead of boys in all 
three areas, on average internationally. Most high-achieving countries had boys higher on knowing than girls, 
while generally having similar scores on the other two areas. However, as in the science content domain, here 
again, almost all Arab-speaking countries had girls achieve higher than boys on all three cognitive areas.

45
CHAPTER 6:
Science achievement, 
ethnicity, and language of 
the home of Year 9 students
 Key Findings
One-fifth of all Year 9 students identified themselves as belonging in more than one ethnic 
grouping; the groupings were Māori, Pasifika, Pākehā/European, Asian, and Other.
There was a wide variation in science achievement within ethnic groupings, with advanced 
achievers and very low achievers in all ethnic groupings.
Māori had lower average science achievement than non-Māori. Similarly, Pasifika Year 9 
students had lower average science achievement than non-Pasifika. There was evidence that 
this achievement gap narrows when socio-economic factors are taken into account.
The average science achievement of Year 9 students classified as Other has decreased 
significantly since 2010/11.
Between 2010/11 and 2014/15, there was a significant increase in the average science 
achievement for Pākehā/European Year 9 girls.
One in five Year 9 students regularly spoke a language other than English at home (most also 
spoke English).
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

In the four year plan of the Ministry of Education, one of its stated priorities is “Māori 
and Pasifika learning and success” (Ministry of Education, 2016). They state that 
“Equitable achievement for Māori, Pasifika and students from low socio-economic 
backgrounds is a major priority which underpins much of our work” (Ministry of 
Education, 2016). While Māori and Pasifika are not a new focus, with previous 
documents and programmes aimed towards these students, it is important to review 
our progress towards this goal. Given that Māori and Pasifika will be an increasingly 
large proportion of New Zealand’s working age population in the future, improving 
the system so that more of them will achieve will contribute to the future wellbeing 
and prosperity of New Zealand (Ministry of Education, 2016).
It is important to interpret this data carefully. It is not the ethnicity of a student, per 
se, that influences outcomes. However, there is clear evidence that when teaching is 
culturally responsive, Māori students, and those of other minority ethnic groupings, 
thrive at school (Bishop et al., 2007). In contrast, Blank, Houkamau, & Kingi (2016) 
suggest that the unconscious bias of teachers lowers their expectations of what 
Māori can achieve. Thus the existence of a relationship between ethnicity and 
achievement, demonstrated in this section, does not imply that being classified in a 
particular ethnic grouping is a cause of poor or good achievement. However, a good 
facility in the language of instruction may be helpful in learning science. 
This section will examine science achievement among ethnic groupings and language 
users. Students were able to identify with more than one ethnic grouping, and so 
results should be read with this overlap in mind. Also note that only English-medium 
students are included in this cycle of TIMSS
46
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Classifying students by ethnicity
Students were asked about their ethnicity by asking “are you...?” and then listing the major ethnic groupings 
in New Zealand. The list also included an opportunity for the student to record any Other grouping they 
might identify with. In previous cycles of TIMSS, New Zealand classified each student to one grouping only, 
using prioritisation. In this cycle, any student who identified with more than one grouping was assigned 
to each of those groupings, in line with the Ethnicity New Zealand Standard Classification (Statistics New 
Zealand, 2005). Students were then assigned to the following groupings based on their responses: Māori; 
Pasifika; Pākehā/European; Asian; and Other. If a student ticked both Māori and Samoan, they would be 
classified as both Māori and Pasifika. Using this methodology, ethnic groupings cannot be compared with 
each other, as 20 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students are in more than one grouping (some are in as 
many as four groupings). Exhibit 6.1 shows how students were classified. There were proportionately more 
Year 9 students in each ethnic grouping using this methodology than if we had used prioritisation.
Exhibit 6.1: Proportion of Year 9 students classified in each ethnic grouping in TIMSS 2014/15
Ethnic grouping
Proportion of Year 9 students

14

73

2
Identified with 2, 3, or 4 groupings
20
The next sections examine the science achievement of Year 9 students within each ethnic grouping, and 
comparisons are only made with those that are NOT in that ethnic grouping.
Science achievement among Māori
As shown in Exhibit 6.2, there was a range of achievement among Māori Year 9 students. The difference 
between high and low achieving Māori was almost the same as non-Māori. However, the whole distribution 
for Māori was lower than for non-Māori, and therefore the average (mean) achievement of Māori was much 
lower than non-Māori. This is consistent with other cycles of TIMSS, and other studies.

Science achievement among Māori girls and boys
As shown in chapter 5, the science achievement of boys overall was the same as girls overall. Similarly, Māori 
Year 9 girls had the same science achievement, on average, as Māori Year 9 boys. This finding was different 
from 2010/11, where Māori boys had higher science achievement, on average, than Māori girls. Between 
2010/11 and 2014/15, there was a small, non-significant increase for Māori Year 9 girls, and a small, non-
significant decrease for Māori Year 9 boys, reducing the difference.
Science achievement among Māori students, taking into account socio-economic 
status
Some preliminary analyses were conducted to see if taking into account the socio-economic status of 
students affected the science achievement of Māori students. Fewer households are buying and storing 
lots of books as the popularity of on-line and electronic reading increases (see chapter 7). However, the 
number of books in the home still has a strong relationship with achievement. These preliminary analyses 
included only books in the home and home possessions, but show that when these measures of socio-
economic status are taken into account, the difference in achievement between Māori and non-Māori is 
smaller (36 scale score points, instead of 59). This finding suggests that ‘being Māori’ does not explain the 
difference in achievement between Māori and non-Māori. Further exploration is warranted to understand 
the difference in achievement between Māori and non-Māori. An understanding of the barriers to learning 
can help break those barriers down, so that Māori students can achieve to their true potential. Further 
exploration should also keep in mind the idea that Māori students face impediments to learning due to 
deficit thinking by teachers (see, for example, Blank et al., 2016).

Science achievement by language
Students were asked how often they spoke the language of instruction at home. In New Zealand, TIMSS 
was only tested in schools where English was the main medium of instruction. This question was formulated 
differently from previous cycles, so no comparisons can be made with these cycles. Nearly 80 percent of 
Year 9 students (79%) indicated that they always spoke English at home, and another 14 percent almost 
always spoke English at home. Nearly all of the rest of the Year 9 students indicated that they sometimes 
spoke English and sometimes another language (6%). Less than one percent of New Zealand Year 9 students 
reported never speaking English at home. Those students who spoke another language, at least a little, most 
commonly spoke an Asian language, or Māori, every day at home. 
Those Year 9 students who sometimes or never spoke English at home most commonly spoke an Asian 
language or a Pasifika language, as shown in Exhibit 6.22. 
Exhibit 6.22: Languages spoken by Year 9 students who sometimes or never spoke English at home in 
TIMSS 2014/15
Other, 6%
2 or more, 9%
Always or
almost always
spoke English
93%
Other, 6%
2 or more, 9%
European, 10%
Māori, 8%
Sometimes or
never spoke
English
Asian, 48%
7%
Pasifika, 19%
Many studies point to the advantages of bilingualism (or indeed multilingualism), including greater flexibility 
of thinking (see, for example, Adesope, Lavin, Thompson, & Ungerleider, 2010). The relationship between 
language used at home and average science achievement in the English-medium TIMSS test was complex. 
Because the science questions in TIMSS include many items that require students to first read the question 
and work out which science is required, a good facility with English is required. Therefore, to fully understand 
the relationship between being bilingual, or multi-lingual, and science achievement, we need to know how 
well the students understand each language.

60
CHAPTER 7:
Science achievement and 
socio-economic status of 
Year 9 students
 Key Findings
Students in homes with many resources for learning had higher science achievement, on 
average, than those whose homes had fewer resources.
Science achievement was higher, on average, for Year 9 students in schools with more affluent 
students than those in schools with more economically disadvantaged students. The difference 
in science achievement between these two groupings within New Zealand was a lot higher 
than the international average.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Possessions and books as measures of socio-economic 
status of students
Home possessions and books in the home can be used to give a measure of both the wealth of the home 
and the level of importance given to education and culture. The TIMSS questionnaires asked lower secondary 
students about the presence in their home of books, and a series of other resources which could be used to 
gauge SES. Arguably, some of these resources could be considered facilitators of educational activities, such 
as an internet connection, but might not necessarily be used as such. This next section will discuss the results 
of these questions.
Books in the home
TIMSS has asked about the number of books in the home since 1994/95. Exhibit 7.1 shows the proportions of 
Year 9 students in three summarised categories of numbers of books in the home, and their average (mean) 
science achievement.
One-third of New Zealand Year 9 students (33%) reported that they had 25 or fewer books in their home. 
Just over one-third of New Zealand Year 9 students (37%) reported that they had more than 100 books in 
their home. This proportion of students with more than 100 books is similar to 2010/11 (40%), but much lower 
than in 1994/95 (66%). It seems likely that fewer households are buying and storing lots of books as the 
popularity of on-line and electronic reading increases.
As shown in Exhibit 7.1, Year 9 students that reported more books in the home had significantly higher 
science achievement, on average, than those with fewer books. This implies that despite fewer households 
buying and storing lots of books, the number of books in the home still has a strong relationship with 
achievement.
The Ministry of Education’s four year plan from 2016 to 2020 states that “Equitable 
achievement for Māori, Pasifika and students from low socio-economic backgrounds 
is a major priority which underpins much of our work” (Ministry of Education, 2016). 
Results from the large-scale international studies, PIRLS, TIMSS, and PISA1, have all 
shown New Zealand has a large disparity in achievement between students with 
high socio-economic status (high SES), and those with low socio-economic status 
(low SES). Schools in New Zealand have received differential funding, based on the 
needs of the communities they are situated in, since TIMSS first began (in 1994), yet 
disparities persist. Therefore, it is important to continue to examine the relationship 
between socio-economic status and achievement of students. This chapter will 
present details of some measures used to examine socio-economic status, along 
with their association with science achievement.
1	
PIRLS is the Progress in International Reading Literacy Study, and has been conducted in 2001, 2005, 2010, & 2015 in New 
Zealand – see http://www.educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pirls.
	
PISA is the Programme for International Student Assessment, and has been conducted in 2000, 2003, 2006, 2009, 2012, & 2015 
in New Zealand – see http://www.educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pisa.

between books in the home and science achievement was still evident within each ethnic grouping.

questions about how to answer this question from students who lived in more than one house as part of 
a shared custody arrangement.

Socio-economic indicators of schools attended
Schools with larger numbers of students from low socio-economic communities are provided with extra 
funding per student. The decile indicator within New Zealand is used to allocate differentiated funding. 
Decile 1 schools are the schools with the highest proportion of students from socio-economically 
disadvantaged communities, while decile 10 schools have the lowest proportion of students from these 
communities. Internationally, there was also information collected from principals that allowed examination 
of the socio-economic status of the school intake. This section will examine these differing measures of 
socio-economic status: decile and composition by student economic background.
Decile
Previous cycles of TIMSS have shown that students attending schools with fewer students from lower socio-
economic backgrounds (having higher decile) had higher average science achievement than those attending 
schools with more students from lower socio-economic backgrounds (from lower decile schools – see 
Caygill, Kirkham, & Marshall, 2013). As shown in Exhibit 7.5, this is also true of the latest cycle of TIMSS, with 
students from higher decile schools (9 and 10) having higher average science achievement (555 scale score 
points) than those from the low decile schools (1 and 2 – 438 scale score points).

There were high achieving Year 9 students in all decile groupings, as measured by the high and advanced

low achieving group (32% of decile 1 and 2 students below low), compared with the high decile groupings

School composition by student economic background
To make international comparisons across schools, there was also information collected from principals 
across the TIMSS countries that allowed examination of the socio-economic status of the combined student 
population in the schools. The School Questionnaire included two questions, one that asked about the 
approximate proportions of students in the school from economically disadvantaged homes, and one that 
asked about the approximate proportions of students from economically affluent homes. Principals were 
asked to choose from four categories: 0 to 10%; 11 to 25%; 26 to 50%; and more than 50%. The international 
researchers combined the responses from these two questions to give a measure of school composition by 
student economic background. The measure created was an index with three categories: schools with more 
affluent than disadvantaged students; schools with neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged students; 
and schools with more disadvantaged than affluent students. This measure allows us to compare the equity 
of our system with other countries.
As shown in Exhibit 7.7, nearly one-third of New Zealand Year 9 students (30%) were in schools with more 
affluent students, while just over one-quarter (28%) were in schools with more disadvantaged students. 
Average science achievement was higher for Year 9 students in schools with more affluent students (550 
scale score points), than those in schools with more economically disadvantaged students (467 scale score 
points). The difference in science achievement between these two groupings within New Zealand (84 scale 
score points) was higher than on average internationally (55). South Africa (165) and Singapore (111) had 
higher differences than New Zealand, while England (86) had a similar difference, and Australia (67) and the 
United States (60) had lower differences between the lower secondary students in more affluent schools, 
and those in more economically disadvantaged schools.

As with the measure of decile, the TIMSS benchmarks show that there are high and low achievers within 
each of these three categories of economic composition of the school population. Exhibit 7.8 presents two 
different ways of looking at this data – those Year 9 students achieving at each of the benchmarks (as shown 
in the graphical part) and those Year 9 students achieving at or above each of the benchmarks (as shown in 
the table part).
There were larger proportions of Year 9 students in the more affluent schools achieving at or above the high 
benchmark (52%), compared with the economically disadvantaged schools (18% of Year 9 students). As 
shown in Exhibit 7.8, there were Year 9 students in each of the three categories of school composition who 
did not demonstrate the ability to complete a reasonable number of the simplest science tasks which TIMSS 
seeks to measure (that is they did not reach the low benchmark). However, there were larger proportions 
of Year 9 students in the more economically disadvantaged schools in this low achieving group (23%), 
compared with the more affluent schools (3%).

TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
69
CHAPTER 8:
Life outside of school for 
Year 9 students learning 
science
 Key Findings
About 60 percent of Year 9 students read for enjoyment daily. Those students that read books, 
e-books or comics for enjoyment more often had significantly higher average science scores than 
those who read less often.
Overall, more Year 9 girls read for enjoyment daily than boys. Within ethnic groupings, fewer 
Māori and Pasifika Year 9 students read daily than non-Māori and non-Pasifika Year 9 students. 
New Zealand Year 9 students were more likely to go to the movies and participate in various 
outdoor activities as a leisure pastime. Doing leisure activities did not appear to have a 
relationship with student science achievement.
New Zealand Year 9 students spent more time on sports as an out of school activity compared 
to other regular activities. Overall, increased time spent on regular out of schools activities was 
associated with lower mean science achievement.
Tutoring in science was less common in New Zealand than in many other countries; only five percent 
of Year 9 students attended extra science lessons or tutoring to excel in class, and nine percent did 
so to keep up in class. 
Most Year 9 students had high educational expectations, with nearly three-quarters expecting 
to do some form of tertiary study. Generally, students with lower educational expectations had 
lower science achievement, on average.
More Year 9 girls had higher expectations than Year 9 boys. Compared with non-Māori, fewer 
Māori students planned to pursue a university education. More Asian students, compared with 
non-Asian students, expected a university education.
Compared to previous cycles, in 2014/15 more Year 9 students expected to complete NCEA Level 2 
or 3, get a national diploma at a tertiary institution, or gain post-graduate diplomas and degrees.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

Activities outside of school are part of students’ everyday life, and play an important 
role in their development as a means of informal learning. In the home context, these 
activities may include independent reading, travelling, exploring, holidaying, and 
many other leisure pastimes. Regular leisure activities like sports, nature, art and 
cultural activities are a big part of the New Zealand lifestyle. Some of these activities 
may boost student achievement; some may provide self-confidence and a vision 
for their future. This chapter will focus on reading for enjoyment, leisure activities 
outside of school, science tutoring outside of school, and students’ educational 
expectations, which may have an effect on student outcomes.
Reading behaviour of Year 9 students
New Zealand Year 9 students were asked how often they spent time reading for enjoyment, to gauge their 
reading habits. Reading resources were grouped in two categories; one included books, e-books, or comics, 
and the second included webpages, blogs or other electronic media. As shown in Exhibit 8.1, the majority 
of Year 9 students (86%) read for enjoyment at least once or twice a week. About three-quarters of Year 
9 students read web pages, blogs or other electronic media, and about two-thirds read books, e-books or 
comics at least once or twice a week. Only nine percent read once or twice a month, and four percent of Year 
9 students reported never or almost never reading for enjoyment (see Exhibit 8.1). 
Reading books, e-books or comics for enjoyment appeared to have a strong relationship with the science 
achievement of Year 9 students; those who read more often had significantly higher scores than those who 
read less often. Reading webpages, blogs or other electronic media did not have as strong a relationship 
with achievement. The Year 9 students who read these web resources daily had significantly higher science 
achievement than those who never or almost never read them. However, there were no significant score 
differences between those Year 9 students in the other categories.

Activities outside of school
New Zealand Year 9 students were asked to report the frequency in which they participated in various one-
off or regular leisure activities outside of school.
Exhibit 8.2 shows that high proportions of Year 9 students have been to the movies and participated in 
various outdoor activities (such as tramping, sailing, skiing, fishing, biking) more than 4 times in the year. 
Having a holiday in another country and camping were the activities that a large proportion of Year 9 
students never participated in over the year. There was no consistent relationship between the frequency of 
doing these activities and the students’ science achievement.

Tutoring in science outside of school
The lower secondary students were asked if they had attended extra lessons or tutoring in science outside 
of school during the last 12 months, and whether they attended to excel in class or to keep up in class. 
They were also asked to report how long they had attended extra tutorials outside of school in the last 12 
months. As Exhibit 8.4 shows, a large proportion of New Zealand Year 9 students (87%) had not attended 
science tutorials outside of school recently, only nine percent did so to keep up in class, and five percent did 
so to excel in class. Most high-performing countries also had few of their lower secondary students attending 
extra lessons or tutoring in science. Asian high-performers like the Republic of Korea, Singapore, Japan and 
Chinese Taipei had bigger proportions of lower secondary students attending science tutorials, whether to 
excel or keep up in class, compared to other high-performing countries. Most of those who took extra tutorials 
had done so for less than four months. Chinese Taipei, Japan and the Republic of Korea were the exceptions, 
where bigger proportions of lower secondary students had had tutoring for more than eight months. 
Perhaps not surprisingly, in most countries, those who did not take extra lessons or tutoring had higher 
achievement than those who did. Japan, Chinese Taipei, the Republic of Korea and Slovenia were exceptions, 
where lower secondary students who had extra tutorials to excel in class had higher science achievement 
than those who did not have extra tutorials, or who had them to keep up in class. In Singapore, Japan and 
Chinese Taipei, the students taking extra tutorials for more than eight months had higher achievement than 
those doing so for shorter periods.
