Education expectations
At the age of 14, many students will not yet have made decisions about their future. However, Year 9 students 
were asked about their intentions for further education. This can give an indication of the importance they 
place on education. Very few Year 9 students expected that they would finish school without some form of 
qualification, with only two percent saying they would only complete up to the end of Year 10, and a further 
four percent who expected to complete only Level 1 of NCEA (see Exhibit 8.5). Most Year 9 students (72%) 
expected to do some form of tertiary study and complete either a certificate, a diploma, or a degree. About 
one-third of Year 9 students (32%) expected to complete some form of post-graduate diploma or degree.
Generally, Year 9 students with lower educational expectations had lower science achievement, on average, 
as shown in Exhibit 8.5. The exception to the general pattern of lower achievement for lower expectations 
was the group of students who expected to gain a PhD; these students had significantly lower science 
achievement than those with other postgraduate tertiary expectations.

across almost all education expectation levels.
Both the gender and ethnic groupings followed a similar pattern of achievement to all the Year 9 students; 
those with higher educational expectations had higher average science scores than those with lower 
expectations.

CHAPTER 9:
Year 9 student attitudes to 
and engagement with science
 Key Findings
More than three-quarters of New Zealand Year 9 students value science and like learning science. 
However, 45 percent of Year 9 students were classified as not confident in science. Compared to the 
international averages, New Zealand had a higher proportion of lower secondary students who do not 
value science, do not like learning science and who were not confident in science.
Overall, Year 9 students who were more positive about science had higher average (mean) achievement 
than those who were less positive. The difference in mean science achievement between the most 
positive and least positive students was larger on the Students confident in science scale than on the 
Students like learning science and Students value science scales.
There was a significant increase in how many New Zealand Year 9 students liked learning science and 
valued science in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11. However, their confidence in science hasn’t changed 
significantly since 2010/11.
New Zealand Year 9 boys were slightly more positive about science than Year 9 girls; more boys like 
learning science and were confident in science, while similar proportions of boys and girls value science. 
The difference in science achievement, on average, between Year 9 boys who were positive about science 
and those who were negative was greater than the difference between the Year 9 girls who were positive 
and the girls who were negative.
Students in the Asian grouping were more positive about science than students in the other ethnic 
groupings.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

In the current fast-changing, hi-tech, economic and social environment, there 
is a growing demand for a more science and technology savvy work force. 
The New Zealand Government’s investment in the national science system 
has grown by over 70 percent between 2007/08 and 2015/16. The National 
Statement of Science Investment “believes excellent, high impact science is 
fundamental to our ability to achieve excellent economic, environmental, social, 
and cultural outcomes for New Zealand” (Ministry of Business Innovation and 
Employment, 2015). It also envisions raising science investment’s contribution 
to the New Zealand economy, and becoming a world-leader in science and 
innovation. This increases emphasis on the role that early education practices 
can play in encouraging students throughout their school years to learn the 
science related subjects, and pursue science majors as their future career. In this 
regard, understanding the influence of different factors on students’ learning and 
achievement in science seems important, and may lead to shaping the practices 
that support confident, connected, actively involved and lifelong learners, as the 
New Zealand Curriculum envisions (Ministry of Education, 2007). 
This chapter looks at students’ attitudes towards learning science, and their 
achievement in the context of how much they like learning science, how much 
they value science, and how confident they are in science subjects.
Student attitudes toward science
To gauge their attitudes towards learning science, Year 9 students were asked how much they agreed with 
a series of 26 statements. Nine of the statements were about how much students liked learning science, 
eight statements were about how confident they were in science, and the rest were about how much they 
valued science. Students were given four response options: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; and 
disagree a lot.
Positive and negative statements were interwoven in the questionnaire, but are reordered in Exhibit 9.1 for 
ease of reading. Of all the statements, New Zealand Year 9 students were most likely to agree that they like 
to conduct science experiments, they learn many interesting things in science and science teaches them 
how things in the world work, with about 86 percent of students agreeing (a lot and a little combined) with 
each of the statements. Most Year 9 students (83%) also agreed that it is important to do well in science. 
While a large proportion of Year 9 students agreed that doing well in science will help them to get into the 
university, polytechnic, or other tertiary institute of their choice (76%), and give them more job opportunities 
when they are adults (79%), half of the Year 9 students indicated that they would not like a job that involves 
using science. 
Half of the Year 9 students indicated that science was not one of their strengths, and about the same 
proportion (52%) disagreed that they are good at working out difficult science problems. Nevertheless, nearly 
three-quarters of the Year 9 students agreed that they usually do well in science. 
The science achievement of Year 9 students had a positive relationship with their attitudes; students with 
more positive attitudes towards science had higher achievement than those with less positive attitudes.

Students like learning science
The Students like learning science scale included the following nine statements:
»»
I enjoy learning science;
»»
I wish I did not have to learn science;
»»
science is boring;
»»
I learn many interesting things in science;
»»
I like science;
»»
I look forward to learning science in school;
»»
science teaches me how things in the world work;
»»
I like to conduct science experiments;
»»
science is one of my favourite subjects.
The lower secondary students were scored according to their degree of agreement with these statements, 
and the results were then categorised into one of three groups: those who very much like learning science; 
those who like learning science; or those who do not like learning science. Note that TIMSS 2010/11 analysis 
used different scale categories - like learning science, somewhat like learning science, and do not like learning 
science (Exhibit 8.2, Martin et al., 2012).
As shown in Exhibit 9.2, a relatively big proportion of New Zealand Year 9 students liked learning science 
(78% - very much like and like combined). Compared to the international average, more New Zealand lower 
secondary students do not like learning science (22%, compared to 19% on average internationally).
The lower secondary students who liked learning science more had higher mean science achievement than those 
who liked it less. The pattern of achievement was similar both for New Zealand and the international average.

Although the international researchers have changed the scale statements and categories since the previous 
cycle, they have calculated an equivalent average scale score that allows us to compare TIMSS 2010/11 and 
2014/15 results. Overall, there was a significant increase in how many New Zealand Year 9 students liked 
learning science in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11 (Exhibit 10.4, Martin et al., 2016).
Students value science
The Students value science scale included the following nine statements: 
»»
I think learning science will help me in my daily life;
»»
I need science to learn other school subjects;
»»
I need to do well in science to get into the university, polytechnic, or other tertiary institute of my choice;
»»
I need to do well in science to get the job I want;
»»
I would like a job that involves using science;
»»
it is important to learn about science to get ahead in the world;
»»
learning science will give me more job opportunities when I am an adult;
»»
my parents think that it is important that I do well in science;
»»
it is important to do well in science.
The students were categorised into one of three groups: those who strongly value; those who value; or those 
who do not value science, according to their responses to these statements. Note that the TIMSS 2010/11 
study used different scale categories – students who value, somewhat value and do not value science 
(Exhibit 8.3, Martin et al., 2012).
As shown in Exhibit 9.3, more than three-quarters of New Zealand Year 9 students valued science (76% - 
value and strongly value categories combined). New Zealand had a slightly lower proportion of secondary 
students who do not value science, compared to the international average (24%, compared to 19% on 
average internationally).
Lower secondary students who valued science more had higher mean achievement than those who valued it 
less. This pattern was similar both in New Zealand, and in many other participating countries. 
Overall, there was a significant increase in how much New Zealand Year 9 students valued science in 2014/15, 
compared to 2010/11 (Exhibit 10.7, Martin et al., 2016)

Students’ confidence in science
The Students confident in science scale included the following eight statements:
»»
I usually do well in science;
»»
science is more difficult for me than for many of my classmates;
»»
science is not one of my strengths;
»»
I learn things quickly in science;
»»
I am good at working out difficult science problems;
»»
my teacher tells me I am good at science;
»»
science is harder for me than any other subject;
»»
science makes me confused.
The students were categorised into one of three groups: very confident; confident; or not confident in 
science, according to their responses to these statements. Note that the TIMSS 2010/11 study used different 
scale categories – students who were confident, somewhat confident and not confident in science (Exhibit 
8.5, Martin et al., 2012).
As shown in Exhibit 9.4, 45 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students were classified as not confident in 
science. New Zealand had more not confident lower secondary students, compared to the international 
average. Many of the high-performing countries also had relatively high proportions of not confident lower 
secondary students (Singapore 44%, Hong Kong SAR 49%, Chinese Taipei 66%, Japan 68% and the Republic 
of Korea 70%). Both in New Zealand and internationally, lower secondary students who were more confident 
in their science abilities had higher science achievement, on average, than those who were less confident.

difference for girls, compared to the Students like learning science (60 and 43 scale score points for boys 
and girls respectively) and the Students value science (66 and 50 scale score points for boys and girls 
respectively) scales.

Attitudes to science by ethnicity
As shown in Exhibit 9.8, the Māori grouping had more Year 9 students (25%) who do not like learning 
science, compared to non-Māori Year 9 students (21%). The Pākehā/European grouping had similar 
proportions on each end of the scale, while having less Year 9 students in the middle category (who like 
learning science) than non- Pākehā/European students. The Asian grouping had a larger proportion of Year 
9 students who like learning science, compared with non-Asian students. There was no disproportionate 
representation of Pasifika students on any scale categories, compared with non-Pasifika students. 
Within each ethnic grouping, there was a consistent pattern of achievement across all scale categories; those 
who liked learning science more achieved higher scores, on average, than those who liked it less.

90
CHAPTER 10:
Year 9 teachers of science
 Key Findings
More New Zealand Year 9 students had science teachers with postgraduate qualifications in 
2014/15, compared to 2010/11 (68% compared to 51%).
Nearly all New Zealand Year 9 students had science teachers who had specialised in science 
and/or science education (93%). On average across all the countries, the same proportion of 
lower secondary students had science teachers with a specialisation in science and/or science 
education.
New Zealand Year 9 science teachers felt less well prepared to teach some topics than others. 
In general, they felt less well prepared to teach topics in Earth science than in other areas.
The majority of New Zealand Year 9 students had teachers who expressed high or very high 
confidence in their own ability to teach science. Of the statements listed, the most confidence 
was expressed in explaining science concepts and principles by doing science experiments, and 
the least confidence was expressed in teaching science using inquiry methods.
Compared to the international average, slightly more New Zealand Year 9 teachers participated 
in science related professional development activities, but fewer New Zealand Year 9 science 
teachers had had more than 35 hours of professional development in the past two years than, 
on average, internationally. 
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

“To ensure every student achieves success our schooling system must provide 
high quality learning opportunities to meet the educational needs of all young 
New Zealanders”(Education Review Office, 2015). Within schools and kura, the 
quality of teaching has the biggest influence on student achievement (Ministry 
of Education, 2016). The New Zealand Government established the Education 
Council of Aotearoa New Zealand in 2015 as a new mandated professional body 
for teachers to lift the status of the teaching profession, and publicly recognise the 
role of quality teaching and educational leadership in raising student achievement. 
Among many tasks, the council is also mandated to develop standards for teachers 
(Ministry of Education, 2016).
To have their practising certificate renewed, teachers are expected to comply with 
the Practising Teacher Criteria (Education Council, n.d.). One criterion requires the 
demonstration of a commitment to ongoing professional learning and development 
of personal professional practice. Schools are responsible for ensuring that teachers 
participate regularly in some form of professional development, the majority of 
which occurs in school contexts.
Along with student achievement, the TIMSS study also collects information about 
teacher education, professional development and experiences. This data may 
not provide enough insight into the connection between student achievement 
and the former, as schools may have different strategies to make use of teaching 
practices and teachers’ expertise. However, it is useful to know if well-educated 
people are being attracted into teaching, whether they are being supported in their 
professional development, and if the schools are actively aiming to retain the highly 
experienced teachers. 
This chapter will report information collected about the characteristics of lower 
secondary teachers, particularly focusing on their education, years of experience, 
preparedness and confidence to teach science, and professional development they 
have undertaken.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

Experience
Nearly one-third of New Zealand Year 9 students had teachers who had 20 years or more teaching 
experience, and 19 percent had teachers with less than five years teaching experience (Exhibit 10.1). On 
average, New Zealand Year 9 science teachers had had 15 years of teaching experience. This has not changed 
significantly since 2010/2011, where the average experience of science teachers was 14 years. 
On average internationally, a similar proportion of lower secondary students had science teachers with 
20 years or more teaching experience. Interestingly, Singaporean lower secondary students had the least 
experienced science teachers, with an average teaching experience of 8 years; Lithuania had the most 
experienced science teachers, with an average teaching experience of 24 years. 
Teachers’ years of experience appeared to have no strong relationship with the science achievement of lower 
secondary students, both in New Zealand and internationally.

Education
As shown in Exhibit 10.2, two-thirds of New Zealand Year 9 students (68%) had science teachers with a 
postgraduate diploma or degree, and 31 percent had science teachers with a bachelor’s degree or equivalent. 
Compared with the previous TIMSS cycle (51% of students in TIMSS 2010/11), more New Zealand Year 9 
students had science teachers with postgraduate or higher qualifications in 2014/15.
New Zealand had a higher proportion of lower secondary science teachers with postgraduate university 
qualifications, compared with the international average.1 However, there were large variations across 
countries; Georgia stood out in comparison to other countries, with 89 percent of their lower secondary 
science teachers having postgraduate qualifications, whereas only 1 percent of the lower secondary science 
teachers in Botswana had postgraduate qualifications. Few Singaporean teachers had postgraduate 
qualifications (18%), but nearly all other Singaporean teachers had a bachelor’s degree (81%).

How well prepared do science teachers feel?
To understand how well prepared the teachers felt to teach science, TIMSS asked about the 22 topics in the 
TIMSS Assessment Frameworks, as shown in Exhibit 10.4 (Hooper et al., 2013). The answer options were: not 
applicable; very well prepared; somewhat prepared; and not well prepared. 
New Zealand Year 9 teachers’ preparedness to teach the science topics varied across the topics. The topics 
that most teachers felt very well prepared to teach were in the area of chemistry, with physical and chemical 
properties of matter (93% of students) the highest. The physics topic electric circuits (flow of current; types 
of circuits - parallel/series) and properties and uses of permanent magnets and electromagnets had only 
44 percent of Year 9 students with teachers who felt very well prepared to teach this topic, the lowest 
proportion of all the topics. In general, the New Zealand Year 9 teachers felt less prepared to teach Earth 
science than other science topics in TIMSS 2014/15. 
Some of the statements had different wording in TIMSS 2010/11, therefore we cannot directly compare 
all statements. Comparing the statements that had similar or almost similar wording in both surveys, the 
proportions of Year 9 students whose teachers felt very well prepared to teach the biology topics appears 
to have increased between 2010/11 and 2014/15. However, the increase was significant for only one topic, 
interdependence of populations of organisms in an ecosystem (e.g. energy flow, food webs, competition, 
predation) and factors affecting population size in an ecosystem, as shown in Exhibit 10.4. In contrast, the 
proportions of Year 9 students whose teachers felt very well prepared to teach the physics topics appears 
to have decreased between 2010/11 and 2014/15. Only one topic, forces and motion (types of forces, basic 
description of motion, effects of density and pressure), had significantly fewer Year 9 students in this cycle 
whose teachers felt very well prepared to teach it (61% of students, compared to 73% in TIMSS 2010/11).
There was no significant relationship between the feelings of preparedness of science teachers and their 
students’ science achievement.

Confidence to teach science
Along with asking about preparedness to teach science content, TIMSS also asked lower secondary science 
teachers to express their level of confidence in doing each of the teaching activities shown in Exhibit 10.5. 
Response options were: very high; high; medium; and low. 
The majority of New Zealand Year 9 science teachers reported having high or very high confidence in doing 
each teaching activity. Fewer reported being confident in teaching science using inquiry methods (46% with 
medium or low confidence), or providing challenging tasks for the highest achieving students (40% with 
medium or low confidence), than other teaching activities. Explaining science concepts and principles by 
doing science experiments was the activity that most Year 9 science teachers were very confident at (very 
high and high combined to make 88% of students). These statements were asked differently in the TIMSS 
2010/11 cycle, therefore it is not possible to draw comparison between the two studies.
Internationally, New Zealand Year 9 science teachers expressed less confidence than many of their peers 
in other countries for some statements, and more for others. Compared with other countries, New Zealand 
Year 9 science teachers expressed relatively low levels of confidence about improving the understanding 
of struggling students, and high levels about explaining science concepts and principles by doing science 
experiments. Japan had the lowest proportions of lower secondary students with teachers who expressed 
having very high confidence in doing any of the listed activities.

Professional development
TIMSS 2014/15 asked teachers about the types of professional development they had participated in during 
the past two years. As shown in Exhibit 10.6, the most common type of professional development in science 
teaching for New Zealand Year 9 teachers was science content (63% of students) and science curriculum 
(60% of students). Integrating information technology into science and science pedagogy/instruction were 
also common professional development areas. The least common topic was science assessment, with only 
41 percent of Year 9 students whose teachers had had professional development in this area.
More New Zealand Year 9 science teachers engaged in professional development in science content, 
curriculum, and integrating information technology into science than their peers internationally. The 
difference between proportions in the other areas was not significant. 
Compared to TIMSS 2010/11, in TIMSS 2014/15 fewer Year 9 students had teachers who did professional 
development in science curriculum (60%, compared to 78% in TIMSS 2010/11).

Teachers were also asked to report the amount of time they spent in formal in-service professional development 
in the last two years. As shown in Exhibit 10.7, 13 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students were taught by 
teachers who had no formal in-service/professional development in the past two years. A further 21 percent 
had teachers who spent less than six hours in in-service/professional development. On average internationally, 
a slightly larger proportion of lower secondary students (17%) had teachers who did not take part in any 
professional development in science in the last two years. However, on average internationally, compared with 
New Zealand, a larger proportion of lower secondary students had teachers who had had more than 35 hours 
of professional development in science in the last two years.

Initial analyses show that the amount of time teachers spent on professional development had no direct affect 
on students’ achievement. The Year 9 students’ achievement was very similar across all categories of time 
teachers spent on professional development. However, this may require further in-depth analysis, with multiple 
factors taken into account, to examine the true nature of the relationship.

TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
99
CHAPTER 11:
Year 9 science teaching 
and learning
 Key Findings
The majority of New Zealand Year 9 students were positive about how their teacher engaged 
with them in their science classes. However, fewer students found their science lessons very 
engaging, compared to the international average. Both in New Zealand and internationally, 
the more engaged the lower secondary students were in their science lessons, the higher the 
achievement.
According to their teachers, New Zealand Year 9 students spent about 3.5 hours on science 
topics per week, which was more than the international average.
New Zealand Year 9 teachers emphasised science investigation less often than many other 
countries. Overall, there was no change in the emphasis on science investigation for Year 9 
students in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11.
Emphasis on science investigation appeared to have no relationship with students’ science 
achievement in New Zealand. Almost all high-achieving countries had a similar pattern to New 
Zealand. However, overall internationally, lower secondary students who were in classes with 
more frequent emphasis on science investigation had higher science achievement than those 
with less frequent emphasis.
New Zealand had a higher proportion of Year 9 students with computers available for use 
during science lessons, compared to other countries. Computers were most commonly used to 
look up ideas and information. 
New Zealand science teachers placed major emphasis on classroom tests for monitoring Year 
9 students’ progress. On average internationally, teachers also placed major emphasis on the 
assessment of students’ ongoing work.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

The quality of teaching is a big focus for the Ministry of Education. Two of the 
four priorities for education for 2016 to 2020 are: “powering up learners, parents, 
communities and employers to influence the quality and relevance of teaching and 
learning and lift achievement”; and “quality teaching, leadership and assessment” 
(Ministry of Education, 2016).
For effective teaching and learning to take place in science, research suggests a 
variety of teaching and learning activities are required (Çimer, 2007). Çimer suggests 
there are six main principles to effective science teaching: 
»»
dealing with students’ existing ideas and conceptions;
»»
encouraging students to apply new concepts or skills into different contexts;
»»
encouraging student participation in lessons;
»»
encouraging student inquiry; 
»»
encouraging co-operative learning among students; 
»»
offering continuous assessment and providing feedback.
Çimer identifies a wide range of teaching methods and learning activities such as 
question-and-answer, discussions (either small group or whole class), small group 
activities, using ICT facilities, practical work, field trips, simulations, writing activities, 
role-play, inquiry-based teaching, and co-operative learning groups. The TIMSS 
study collects a vast array of contextual data to help analyse the factors that may 
contribute to students’ learning and achievement.
This chapter will report information about students’ engagement in their science 
lessons, teachers’ perspectives on instructional methods and learning techniques, 
the ways student progress is measured, and the use of technological resources for 
science learning.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

Back to Contents

Science teaching at the Year 9 level
According to their teachers, New Zealand Year 9 students spent an average of 207 minutes on science topics 
per week. The instructional time on science ranged from 50 minutes to over 5.5 hours per week in New 
Zealand. The average international instructional time was 167 minutes per week.
New Zealand Year 9 teachers were also asked about the availability of assistance and experts in science 
teaching. Twelve percent of Year 9 students were in classes which undertook science projects with someone 
from outside their school, to help the students learn specific science concepts. Additionally, 12 percent 
of Year 9 students also had someone from outside their school come and teach them a specific science 
concept during their science classes. The questions didn’t mention the frequency of this happening. 
Students’ perspectives on teachers’ efforts to engage them 
in science lessons
Year 9 students were asked their agreement to ten statements, shown in Exhibit 11.1, to gauge their level of 
engagement with their science lessons. Their answer options were: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; 
and disagree a lot. Overall, New Zealand Year 9 students were positive in their responses about how engaged 
they were in their science lessons, with the majority of students agreeing (a lot and a little combined) to 
almost all statements. The most commonly agreed upon aspect of science learning engagement was I know 
what my teacher expects me to do, with 88 percent of Year 9 students agreeing (a lot and a little combined) 
to it. The least agreed aspect was my teacher lets me show what I have learned (71%).
Overall, there was a positive relationship between the Year 9 students’ science achievement and their 
agreement to the engagement statements; those who agreed more to the statements had significantly 
higher scores than those who agreed less.

The international researchers combined the responses to the statements in Exhibit 11.1 into the Students’ 
views on engaging teaching in science lessons scale. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values were 
grouped into three categories: very engaging teaching; engaging teaching; and less than engaging teaching. 
The majority of lower secondary students, both in New Zealand and internationally, reported that their 
science lessons were engaging (engaging and very engaging combined). However, fewer New Zealand Year 
9 students reported experiencing very engaging teaching in science lessons than on average internationally 
(see Exhibit 11.2). Both the New Zealand and the international average show that the more engaged the lower 
secondary students were in their science lessons, the higher the achievement.

Science teachers’ perspectives on instructional techniques 
Teachers were asked to report how often they used certain instructional techniques. As Exhibit 11.3 shows, 
67 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students were in classes where science teachers encouraged students to 
express their ideas, every or almost every lesson. Another common instructional technique that teachers used 
every or almost every lesson was linking new content to students’ prior knowledge (60%). The least common 
activity was asking students to decide their own problem solving procedures, with only nine percent of Year 
9 students having teachers who used this technique every or almost every lesson, and 35 percent who had 
their science teacher use it about half the lessons. There was no consistent relationship between teachers’ 
use of instructional techniques and student science achievement.

Science teachers’ perspectives on learning activities
In addition to instructional techniques, teachers were also asked to report on the extent to which they used 
certain learning activities in their science lessons. The fifteen learning activities, shown in Exhibit 11.4, had 
answer options: never; some lessons; about half the lessons; and every or almost every lesson. Because there 
were fewer than 10 percent of Year 9 students doing most of the activities every or almost every lesson, the 
results are combined for reporting the data on about half the lessons and every or almost every lesson into 
one category, called about half the lessons or more often.
In TIMSS 2014/15, two of the most frequently used learning activities by New Zealand teachers were asking 
students to listen to them explain new science content, and conducting experiments or investigations, with 
89 and 70 percent of Year 9 students respectively having their teacher use these activities about half the 
lessons or more often during their science lessons. As reported by the teachers, New Zealand Year 9 students 
were more likely to work in mixed ability groups in their science lessons than work in same ability groups; 57 
percent of students worked in mixed ability groups about half the lessons or more often, as opposed to 21 
percent in same ability groups. Students were also less likely to do field work outside of class (28%) and have 
to memorise facts and principles (12%), with relatively big proportions of Year 9 students having teachers 
who never use these learning activities in their science class.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

Nine out of the fifteen activities were also included in the TIMSS 2010/11 study. The comparison of results 
from the two studies shows no overall significant change in the use of the learning activities, except for one; 
the teachers were less likely to have students memorise facts and principles in 2014/15 than in 2010/11. The 
proportion of Year 9 students whose teacher used this learning activity about half the lessons or more often 
decreased from 32 percent in the previous cycle, to 23 percent in TIMSS 2014/15.

Emphasis on science investigation
To summarise how often teachers used science investigation in their lessons, the international researchers 
combined the responses to the eight statements listed below into the Teachers emphasise science 
investigation scale with two categories: about half the lessons or more; and less than half the lessons. 
»»

»»
watch the teacher demonstrate an experiment or investigation;
»»

»»
conduct experiments or investigations;
»»

»»
interpret data from experiments or investigations;
»»
use evidence from experiments or investigations to support conclusions;
»»
do field work outside of class. 
The majority of lower secondary students, both in New Zealand and in many high-achieving countries, were 
in classes where science investigation was emphasised less than half the lessons (see Exhibit 11.5). Kazakhstan 
stood out with bigger proportion of lower secondary students (39%) whose teachers emphasised science 
investigation in about half the lessons or more.

»

The teachers’ emphasis on science investigation appeared to have no relationship with students’ science 
achievement in New Zealand. Almost all high-achieving countries had a similar pattern to New Zealand. 
However, overall internationally, those lower secondary students who were in classes where science 
investigation was emphasised in about half the lessons or more had higher achievement than those in the 
other category.
Use of computers
Compared to other countries, New Zealand were among those with a higher proportion of lower secondary 
students (60%) whose teachers responded that computers were available for use during science lessons. 
On average internationally, 42 percent of lower secondary students were in classes where computers 
were available for use during science lessons (see Exhibit 11.6). New Zealand Year 9 students mainly used 
computers to look up ideas and information, with 56 percent of students using the computer for this purpose 
at least monthly. Using computers for doing scientific procedures or experiments was less common, with 26 
percent of New Zealand Year 9 students using computers for these purposes at least monthly. 
In terms of the overall level of accessibility of computers at school, 18 percent of New Zealand Year 9 
students had teachers report that students had access to shared computers in the classroom. Fifty-
six percent of students had teachers who reported that the school had computers the class could use 
sometimes, and only 14 percent of Year 9 students had access to their own computer at school. 
There was no significant difference between the achievement of students who had computers available to 
use during their science lessons and those who did not.

Monitoring student progress 
According to the National Administration Guidelines (NAGs), schools in New Zealand are required to gather 
information that is sufficiently comprehensive to enable the progress and achievement of students to be 
evaluated through a range of assessment practices (Ministry of Education, 2015). Teachers were asked how 
much emphasis they placed on different forms of monitoring students’ progress in science (see Exhibit 11.7). 
The New Zealand Year 9 teachers mainly used classroom tests, with 74% of students studying in classes 
where teachers placed major emphasis on this form of monitoring tool. Internationally, besides classroom 
tests (73%), the assessment of students’ ongoing work was also a common form of monitoring student 
progress in science, with 69 percent of lower secondary students’ progress being monitored using this tool. 
The emphasis on different methods of monitoring students’ progress seemed to have no relationship with 
student science achievement.

108
CHAPTER 12:
Year 9 students’ 
perceptions of climate for 
learning
 Key Findings
Seven out of ten New Zealand Year 9 students were positive about their schools, teachers and 
classmates, and nearly half had a high sense of school belonging. Students who had a high 
sense of belonging had higher science achievement, on average, than those with less of a sense 
of belonging.
There were more Year 9 students in this latest cycle, compared with 2010/11, agreeing that they 
feel safe at school, like being in school, and think that students at their school care about each 
other.
New Zealand Year 9 students experienced bullying behaviours at school more frequently than 
many other participating countries. Compared to 2010/11, more Year 9 students experienced 
the individual bullying behaviours.
More Year 9 boys reported they experienced bullying behaviours than Year 9 girls. More 
Pākehā/European students reported they experienced bullying behaviours than non-Pākehā/
European students. There was no difference in the proportions experiencing bullying 
behaviours between Māori and non-Māori, between Pasifika and non-Pasifika, or between 
Other and non-Other students. Fewer Asian students reported experiencing bullying 
behaviours than non-Asian. More Year 9 students experienced bullying behaviours in schools 
with lower socio-economic status. 
Those Year 9 students experiencing bullying behaviours more often had lower science 
achievement, on average, than those experiencing them less often
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

A large body of international and local research indicates a close connection 
between school climate and academic achievement, as well as the behavioural and 
social-emotional wellbeing of students (Boyd & Barwick, 2011; Thapa et al., 2012; 
O’Malley et al., 2012; Ruus et al., 2007; Macneil et al., 2009; Voight et al., 2013; 
Marsh et al., 2014). Students’ perceptions of school climate appear to be positively 
associated with their academic motivation, sense of school belonging, attitudes 
towards learning, and positive social behaviours and attitudes in general. Therefore, 
it is important to study the factors that influence how students feel about their 
school learning environment.
TIMSS collects a vast amount of contextual data, including students’ responses to 
questions about their school. This chapter examines students’ perceptions of the 
school climate, in the context of their sense of school belonging and experience of 
bullying behaviours within the school environment.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9

Students’ sense of school belonging
To examine the relationship and connection the students have with their school, teachers and classmates, 
they were asked seven positive agree/disagree statements (see Exhibit 12.1). In addition, New Zealand Year 
9 students were also asked if they agreed with a further statement, I think that students at this school care 
about each other. The response options to all statements were: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; and 
disagree a lot. 
As Exhibit 12.1 shows, most New Zealand Year 9 students were positive about their school, teachers and 
classmates, with at least seven out of ten students agreeing with each of these statements. The statement 
with the highest level of agreement was I like to see my classmates at school, with 95 percent of students 
agreeing (a lot and a little combined); this also had the largest proportion of Year 9 students agreeing a lot 
with the statement. The next most agreed with statements were I learn a lot at school, I feel safe when I am 
at school, and I am proud to go to this school. Interestingly though, I think that students at this school care 
about each other was the least agreed statement, with 70 percent of students agreeing with it.

To understand students’ sense of belonging better, the international researchers combined seven of the 
statements in Exhibit 12.1 to create the student’s Sense of school belonging scale. As mentioned earlier, the 
eighth statement, I think that students at this school care about each other, was only asked in New Zealand. 
To report the scale in a meaningful way, students were grouped into three categories: little sense of belonging; 
sense of belonging; and high sense of belonging.

As shown in Exhibit 12.2, 43 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students had a high sense of school belonging; 
49 percent had a sense of school belonging, and a smaller proportion of Year 9 students had little sense of 
school belonging (8%). These proportions were very similar to the international averages.
New Zealand Year 9 students with a high sense of belonging had significantly higher science achievement 
than those in the middle category (sense of belonging). Similarly, those students with a sense of belonging 
at school had higher science achievement than those with little sense of belonging (see Exhibit 12.2). This 
pattern was also evident on average internationally; the higher the sense of belonging, the higher the science 
achievement of students.

Students’ sense of school belonging by gender
The proportion of Year 9 girls with a high sense of belonging was not significantly different from the 
proportion of Year 9 boys in this same category. Similarly, there were no significant differences between the 
proportion of girls and boys in the other two categories of sense of school belonging. Year 9 girls and boys 
with a higher sense of school belonging had higher science achievement, on average, than those with a lower 
sense of belonging. Interestingly, the difference between boys with a high sense of belonging and boys with 
little sense of belonging was larger, on average, than this difference for girls.

Students’ sense of school belonging by ethnicity
Reflective of all New Zealand Year 9 students, all ethnic groupings had between 35 and 50 percent of their 
students having a high sense of school belonging, and less than 10 percent with little sense of belonging. 
Fewer Māori Year 9 students had a high sense of belonging, compared with non-Māori; all other ethnic 
groupings had similar proportions of Year 9 students who had a high sense of belonging, compared 
with their non-identified ethnic grouping (Exhibit 12.4). More Pākehā/European students had little sense 
of belonging than non- Pākehā/European students. In contrast, fewer Asian students had little sense of 
belonging than non-Asian students. 
In general, Year 9 students who had a higher sense of belonging achieved higher science scores than those 
who had a lower sense of belonging, across all ethnic groupings. However, for some of the ethnic groupings, 
the differences were not significant between those with a high sense of belonging, and those categorised as 
just having a sense of belonging (not high or little).

Trends in students’ perceptions of school belonging – TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15
Comparing the school belonging statements that were asked during the previous TIMSS cycle shows that 
there was an increase in the proportion of Year 9 students who agreed with these statements in 2014/15, 
compared to 2010/11 (see Exhibit 12.5). The only exception was for the statement I feel like I belong at this 
school, for which the proportion didn’t change.

Students’ experiences of bullying behaviours at school 
A sense of security at school promotes a stable learning environment. Bullying among students is a threat 
to this environment and causes distress to victims, leads to low self-esteem, a decreased sense of belonging 
and poor achievement (Hooper et al., 2013). To find out how often they experienced any bullying behaviour 
during the year, Year 9 students were asked nine statements, as shown in Exhibit 12.6. Their response options 
were: never; a few times a year; once or twice a month; and at least once a week.
The most commonly reported bullying behaviour among New Zealand Year 9 students was being made 
fun of or called names, with 38 percent of students reporting they experienced this behaviour once or 
twice a month or more frequently during the school year. The cyber-bullying statement someone posted 
embarrassing things about me online (new in 2014/15), was the least commonly reported behaviour, with only 
seven percent of Year 9 students reporting this happened once or twice a month or more frequently.
For all statements, the science achievement of New Zealand Year 9 students was higher for those who 
experienced the bullying behaviour less frequently (i.e. responded with a few times a year or never), 
compared to those who experienced the bullying behaviour at least once or twice a month. Some of the 
greatest differences in mean science achievement were seen for the least commonly experienced behaviours 
(see Exhibit 12.6).

Standard errors are presented in parentheses.
To describe the extent to which Year 9 students experienced bullying behaviours at school, their responses 
to these statements were combined into the Students bullied at school scale, and the values on the scale 
were grouped into three categories: almost never; about monthly; and about weekly. This data should be 
read with caution, as the scale is based on individual behaviours and could range from a repeated behaviour 
on a weekly basis from a group of individuals, to four different behaviours, with each behaviour happening 
on separate occasions only once a month, and instigated by different perpetrators. However, this still 
indicates the frequency students experienced negative behaviours. 
New Zealand Year 9 students were more likely to experience bullying behaviours at school than lower 
secondary students in many TIMSS 2014/15 countries. On the Students bullied at school scale, 45 percent 
of New Zealand Year 9 students reported they experienced bullying behaviours at school about monthly or 
more often, compared to 37 percent on average internationally (Exhibit 12.7). New Zealand Year 9 students 
did not stand out as much on this scale as the New Zealand Year 5 students, where 60 percent of Year 5 
students reported they experienced bullying behaviours at school at least monthly, compared to 44 percent 
on average internationally (see Exhibit 12.7 Caygill, Singh, & Hanlar, 2016).
New Zealand Year 9 students who almost never experienced bullying behaviours had similar science 
achievement, on average, as those who experienced it about monthly, while both had significantly higher 
achievement than those who experienced it about weekly. Internationally, lower secondary students 
who experienced bullying more frequently had lower science achievement, on average, than those who 
experienced it less frequently. Some of the high-performing countries, Chinese Taipei, Kazakhstan, the 
Republic of Korea, Japan, and Hong Kong SAR, were the exceptions, where the frequency of experiencing 
bullying behaviours at school had no relationship with achievement results (they had similar achievement 
across all categories of the scale).

Trends in students’ perceptions about school safety – TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15
The questions given to lower secondary students about school safety have changed since TIMSS was first 
implemented in 1994/95. The best comparison can be made between TIMSS 2014/15 and 2010/11 results, as 
six out of nine of the statements about students experiencing bullying behaviours at school were asked in 
both cycles, and although the wording was slightly different, these statements had the same answer options. 
As Exhibit 12.8 shows, there was an increase in the proportion of Year 9 students who reported experiencing 
the bullying behaviours once a month or more often in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11 results. Although the 
changes were small, they were statistically significant.

Experiences of bullying behaviours among New Zealand Year 9 boys and girls
As shown in Exhibit 12.9, more New Zealand Year 9 girls almost never experienced bullying behaviours, 
compared with Year 9 boys. For girls, those who experienced bullying behaviour more frequently had 
significantly lower mathematics achievement than those who experienced it less frequently. For boys, those 
who almost never experienced bullying behaviours had about the same mean science achievement as those 
who experienced them about monthly, but both were higher than those who experienced the behaviours 
about weekly.

Experiences of bullying behaviours among different ethnic groupings 
of Year 9 students
Reflective of all New Zealand Year 9 students, all ethnic groupings had more than half of their students 
almost never experiencing bullying behaviours, and around 10 percent experiencing these behaviours about

Experiences of bullying behaviours within the different socio-economic 
status schools
As shown in chapter 7, the socio-economic status of students has a strong relationship with their 
achievement. TIMSS 2014/15 asked school principals to report on the economic composition of their school 
by estimating the proportion of students from economically disadvantaged and economically affluent 
homes. As reported in Chapter 7, schools were categorised as being more affluent if more than a quarter of 
their students were from economically affluent homes, and not more than a quarter were from economically 
disadvantaged homes. More disadvantaged schools had the opposite, more than a quarter of their students 
were from economically disadvantaged homes, and not more than a quarter from economically affluent 
homes. All the remaining schools were classified as neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged.
As shown in Exhibit 12.11, more affluent and neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools had 
the same proportions of Year 9 students experiencing bullying behaviours. More disadvantaged schools, 
however, had a bigger proportion of Year 9 students who experienced bullying behaviours about weekly, and 
a smaller proportion who almost never experienced them, compared with more affluent schools.
The different socio-economic status schools followed the general pattern of New Zealand Year 9 students on 
the bullying scale; those who experienced bullying behaviours about weekly had significantly lower science 
scores, on average, than those who experienced them less frequently.

TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
121
CHAPTER 13:
Year 9 science teachers’ 
perceptions of climate 
for learning
 Key Findings
Compared to 2010/11, more Year 9 science teachers characterised students’ desire to do well at 
school as high in their school in 2014/15.
According to science teachers, more than half of Year 9 students were in schools with high or 
very high emphasis on academic success; this proportion was slightly higher than, on average, 
internationally. Higher school emphasis on academic success was associated with higher 
science scores, both in New Zealand, and internationally.
Forty percent of the Year 9 students were taught science by teachers who were categorised as 
very satisfied with their job; however 93 percent of teachers were proud of the work they did. 
The proportion of Year 9 students taught science by very satisfied teachers was lower in New 
Zealand than, on average, internationally.
The proportion of New Zealand Year 9 students who had science teachers who felt their school 
was very safe and orderly was higher in New Zealand than, on average, internationally. Higher 
science achievement was associated with higher school safety.
Students who studied in classes where teachers felt their science teaching was very limited or 
somewhat limited by student needs had lower science achievement than those who studied 
in classes where teachers felt their teaching was not limited at all by student needs. More New 
Zealand teachers, compared with the international average, reported that student needs did 
not limit their science teaching.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

Cohen et al. (2009) define school climate as the quality and character of school 
life. They envisage a productive school climate as one where students, families, and 
educators work together to develop, live, and contribute to a shared school vision. 
They determine four essential dimensions of school climate: safety; teaching and 
learning; relationships; and environmental-structural. 
According to the Ministry of Education (2016), within schools and kura, the quality 
of teaching has the biggest influence on student achievement. The TIMSS 2010/11 
results showed higher achievement for schools that provided good working 
conditions for teachers (Hooper et al., 2013). Johnson (2006) and Johnson et al. 
(2012) state that “a manageable workload, adequate facilities, and the availability 
of instructional materials are important ingredients to fostering productive working 
conditions and promoting teacher satisfaction” (as cited in Hooper et al., 2013). 
Caprara et al. (2006) attests that a positive school culture and environment can 
lead to greater teacher job satisfaction and self-efficacy, which in turn can increase 
student learning (in Hooper et al., 2013). “An orderly environment makes it possible 
for teachers to focus on teaching and students to focus on learning” (Robinson, 
Hohepa, & Lloyd, 2009).
To explore teachers’ perceptions of both the physical and social school 
environments, TIMSS collected data from them on aspects of their schools. 
This included data on teachers’ job satisfaction, teacher interactions, teachers’ 
perceptions of school safety, limitations to science teaching and challenges facing 
teachers. This chapter presents the findings of these analyses.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

School emphasis on academic success
Lower secondary teachers were asked to characterise the different aspects of school life within their 
school that contribute to academic success. The statements varied from teachers, parents and students’ 
involvement in learning and school life, to teacher’s job satisfaction, as listed in Exhibit 13.1. They were given 
five response options: very high; high; medium; low; and very low.
Out of all the 18 statements listed, the aspect of schooling rated highly by the most New Zealand science 
teachers was teachers’ understanding of the school’s curricular goals, with 88 percent of Year 9 students 
having teachers who felt that teachers had very high or high understanding of the curricular goals within their 
school. The more highly rated statements were generally about teachers’ characteristics and role in learning. 
The statements least often highly rated were statements about parents; more than half of the Year 9 students’ 
science teachers thought that parental support for student achievement, parental commitment to ensure that 
students are ready to learn, and parental involvement in school activities were not high within their school.

The TIMSS 2014/15 teacher questionnaire included 11 new statements that were not asked in previous cycles, 
but seven out of the 18 statements were included in the TIMSS 2010/11 study as well. As shown in Exhibit 
13.2, although it appears that quite a few of the aspects of school climate have changed since 2010/11, only 
one was a significant change. The only significant change was science teachers’ characterisation of students’ 
desire to do well at school, which increased from 37 percent of Year 9 students whose teacher categorised 
this as high or very high in 2010/11, to 48 percent in 2014/15.

The international researchers combined the responses to 14 out of the 18 statements into the School 
emphasis on academic success scale (see Exhibit 13.3). TIMSS indicators of school emphasis on academic 
success included school administrators’ and teachers’ expectations for successful curriculum implementation 
and student achievement, parental support for student achievement, and the student’s desire to achieve. 
Therefore, the scale didn’t include the statements about teachers’ job satisfaction, clarity of the school’s 
educational objectives, amount of instructional support provided to teachers by school leadership, and school 
leadership’s support for teachers’ professional development. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values 
were grouped into three categories: very high; high; and medium emphasis. As the majority of responses 
were very positive, there was no low or very low category in the scale.
As shown in Exhibit 13.3, more than half of New Zealand schools (57%) had high or very high emphasis on 
academic success, according to the Year 9 science teachers. Compared to the international average, slightly 
more New Zealand schools had high emphasis on academic success. 
Higher school emphasis on academic success, as reported by the teachers, was associated with higher 
achievement scores in TIMSS science for lower secondary students, both in New Zealand and internationally, 
even though the score difference between the students of very high and high emphasis schools was not 
significant for New Zealand. The science achievement difference between lower secondary students whose 
schools had very high emphasis on academic success, and those whose schools had medium emphasis, was 
about the same in New Zealand as, on average, internationally (50 score points in New Zealand, compared with 
49 score points, on average, internationally).

Science teachers’ job satisfaction
To better understand teachers’ satisfaction with their jobs, they were asked seven statements about their 
feelings towards being a teacher, as shown in Exhibit 13.4. The answer options to the question “How often do 
you feel the following way about being a teacher?” were: very often; often; sometimes; and never or almost 
never. However, due to the very small percentage of never or almost never responses, the graph shows this 
category combined with sometimes as sometimes or less often. 
Overall, New Zealand Year 9 science teachers felt very positive about being teachers. Ninety-three percent 
of Year 9 students had teachers who were very often or often proud of the work they do. Most teachers 
were also enthusiastic about their job (86% of Year 9 students very often or often), and found their work 
full of meaning and purpose (85% of students very often or often). Less than two percent of Year 9 science 
teachers replied never or almost never to nearly all of the statements in Exhibit 13.4. The statement I am 
going to continue teaching for as long as I can was the least frequently experienced feeling about being a 
teacher, with five percent of teachers who never or almost never felt this way, and 76 percent, the lowest 
among all seven statements, who felt it very often or often.

On the basis of responses to these statements, the international researchers created a Teacher job satisfaction 
scale. This scale combines all the responses into three categories: very satisfied; satisfied; and less than satisfied.
As shown in Exhibit 13.5, 40 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students were taught by teachers who were 
very satisfied with their job, and 47 percent were taught by teachers who were satisfied. The New Zealand 
proportion of very satisfied teachers was smaller than the international average proportion (49%). There was 
not a strong relationship between teachers’ job satisfaction and their students’ achievement. Internationally, 
the pattern differed from country to country, but there was not a strong relationship in a lot of countries.

Interactions between science teachers
Teachers, as professionals, spend time learning and improving their practice throughout their careers. An 
excellent way to learn is through interactions and collaborations with other teachers. TIMSS asked teachers 
how often they interact with other teachers, as learners, collaborators, or teachers, as shown in Exhibit 13.6. 
Four possible response options for the frequency of interactions were: very often; often; sometimes; and 
never or almost never. 
The most common type of interaction was discussing how to teach a particular topic, with more than two-
thirds of Year 9 students having science teachers who reported having these interactions very often or often 
(see Exhibit 13.6). Visiting another classroom to learn more about teaching was the least frequently practiced 
interaction by a considerable margin. The frequency of teacher interactions appeared to have no link with 
Year 9 students’ science achievement.

Science teachers’ perceptions of school safety and student 
behaviours
To learn what teachers think about their school safety and student behaviour in their schools, science 
teachers of Year 9 students were asked to indicate the extent to which they agreed or disagreed with eight 
statements on the general levels of safety they experienced at their schools (see Exhibit 13.7). There were 
four possible response options given: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; and disagree a lot. 
Almost all Year 9 students were taught by science teachers who agreed (a lot and a little combined) their 
school was a safe place, with 97 percent agreeing with the statement I feel safe at this school. The least 
agreed statement was the students respect school property, with 29 percent of Year 9 students having 
teachers disagree to some extent with this. Five of the eight statements were asked in the TIMSS 2010/11 
cycle as well. Comparing the responses to these statements in each cycle shows no significant change in 
their proportions.

International researchers summarised teachers’ responses to these statements into a scale called the Safe 
and orderly school scale. This scale describes the extent to which teachers felt their school was a safe and 
orderly environment with three value categories: very safe and orderly; safe and orderly; and less than safe 
and orderly. 
As shown in Exhibit 13.8, just over half of New Zealand Year 9 students (53%) had teachers who agreed that 
their school was very safe and orderly, which was higher than the international average of 45 percent. Both in 
New Zealand and internationally, lower secondary students had higher science achievement in schools where 
the level of safety was higher.

Challenges facing science teachers
To find out about the challenges facing Year 9 science teachers, they were asked to rate their agreement 
with the statements listed in Exhibit 13.9 using: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; and disagree a lot 
as answer options. The most common challenge that the Year 9 teachers were facing was a lack of time to 
assist individual students, with 46 percent of students having science teachers agree a lot, and 44 percent 
agree a little to this statement. Having too many administrative tasks (72%) and needing more time to 
prepare for class (73%) were also amongst the most commonly experienced challenges, with more than 70 
percent of teachers agreeing a lot or a little to this statement. The Year 9 science teachers were less likely 
to report experiencing too much pressure from parents or too many teaching hours, compared to other 
challenges.

The international researchers combined the responses to these statements into a scale called Challenges 
facing teachers. According to the scale, seven percent of New Zealand Year 9 students had science teachers 
who faced many challenges in their teaching, while 41 percent faced few challenges (see Exhibit 13.10). 
Internationally, a similar proportion of lower secondary teachers were facing many challenges in their 
teaching. Within New Zealand, there was no relationship between science teacher reports of challenges and 
the science achievement of their students. Although there was a relationship observed internationally, the 
difference in achievement between those students whose teachers were facing many challenges, and those 
whose teachers were facing few challenges, was relatively small (14 scale score points).

The bars on the graph represent the proportions of lower secondary students. The points represent the mean scores, and the 
lines extending from the points represent the 95% confidence interval, i.e. the range in which we are 95% confident that the 
true population value lies.
	
Few challenges on the scale corresponded to teachers “disagreeing a little” with at least four of the eight statements, and 
“agreeing a little” with the rest, on average. Many challenges corresponded to them “agreeing a lot” with at least four of the 
eight statements, and “agreeing a little” with the rest, on average. All other cases were grouped under some challenges.
Source:	 Exhibit 6.9, Martin, Mullis, Foy, & Hooper, 2016.

Limitations to science teaching
In addition to asking about challenges facing teachers, TIMSS asked lower secondary teachers to describe to 
what extent the factors listed in Exhibit 13.11 limited science teaching in their classes. Response options were: 
not at all; some; and a lot. 
The most common limitation was students lacking prerequisite knowledge or skills, with 84 percent of Year 9 
students having science teachers who thought this factor put some or a lot of limitations on how they taught 
their class. The factors like disruptive students and students suffering from not enough sleep were also 
largely reported as putting a lot or some limitations on science teaching. Students with physical disabilities 
seemed to be the least limiting factor; 88 percent of Year 9 students had teachers who reported that this 
did not limit their teaching at all. However, the research didn’t collect data on what percentage of teachers 
had students with physical disabilities in their class. The fact that not every class has these types of students 
might be an explanation why this wouldn’t be a limiting factor for many teachers.

The international researchers combined the responses to six of the statements into a scale called Teaching 
limited by student needs. The statement students with physical disabilities was not included in the scale. 
The scale had three degrees of limitation: not limited; somewhat limited; and very limited.
According to this scale, 37 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students had teachers who reported that their 
science teaching was not limited by student needs (see Exhibit 13.12). This was higher than the international 
average; on average, 28 percent of teachers internationally reported that student needs did not limit their 
science teaching. Five percent of New Zealand Year 9 students studied in an environment where teachers felt 
their teaching was very limited due to students’ needs.
Limitations to teaching appeared to have strong relationship with student science achievement; the more 
teaching was limited by student needs, the lower the achievement. A similar pattern was observed for many 
other countries as well, as shown by the international average.

Class size
TIMSS asked teachers about the size of their classes, as larger or smaller classes can influence how the 
teacher chooses to teach science topics. The average TIMSS class size for science at the Year 9 level in 
New Zealand was 26 students in 2014/15. The international average class size was 29 students. In the 
majority of countries, average class size ranged from 20 to 35 students; South Africa (46), Egypt (43), 
Botswana (39), Morocco (38), and Singapore (36) were the few exceptions. 
It is difficult to draw a conclusion about the relationship between class size and achievement. For example, in 
some countries smaller classes tend to be in rural areas where there are fewer resources, and larger classes 
in urban areas with more resources. Remedial classes may also be smaller. However, TIMSS shows that 
high-performing Asian countries have relatively larger class sizes (ranging from 31 to 36 students in TIMSS 
2014/15), while most non-Asian top-performing countries tend to have smaller class sizes (between 21 and 26 
students in TIMSS 2014/15). 
Class sizes in New Zealand have not changed significantly since the first cycle of TIMSS; in 1994/95, the 
average class size was 25 for science, 26 in 2010/11, and 26 in 2014/15.

135
CHAPTER 14:
Year 9 principals’ 
perceptions of climate 
for learning
 Key Findings
Overall, the principals were very positive about their teachers’ role in the school’s academic 
success. They were, however, less positive about parents’ support and involvement than 
about their teachers and students. The principals and science teachers had similar views on 
the aspects of the school’s academic success that involved teachers. However, a significant 
difference was observed in those that involved student efforts and parental involvement; the 
principals were more positive than teachers about these aspects.
Overall, principals’ perceptions of their school’s academic climate don’t appear to have 
changed much since 2002/03. However, a significant increase was observed in principals’ 
perceptions of teachers’ degree of success in implementing the school’s curriculum, and 
teachers’ expectations for student achievement. 
More New Zealand schools reported high or very high emphasis on academic success, 
compared to the international average, and to most high-achieving countries. New Zealand 
Year 9 students studying in schools with a higher emphasis on academic success had higher 
science achievement, on average. 
Compared to other countries, including most of the high-achieving countries, New Zealand 
lower secondary school principals were less positive about school discipline. This was in line 
with student reports of bullying behaviours at school; New Zealand had a relatively higher 
proportion of Year 9 students regularly experiencing bullying behaviours. However, more New 
Zealand schools had hardly any problems with school discipline in 2014/15, compared with 
TIMSS 2010/11. Being in a school with less of a problem with school discipline was associated 
with higher achievement in science, both in New Zealand and, on average, internationally.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

Principals may not be part of classroom teaching, but they have a strong, indirect 
influence on educational achievement. Hitt and Tucker (2015) established five 
essential broad domains of effective leader practices that positively align with 
student achievement: establishing and conveying the vision; facilitating a high-
quality learning experience for students; building professional capacity; creating 
an effective and supportive environment for learning; and connecting with external 
partners. As part of the Investing in Educational Success programme, the Ministry 
of Education introduced a Principal Recruitment Allowance to help New Zealand’s 
most high-need schools and kura attract highly-effective principals who can provide 
the leadership needed to lift student achievement (Ministry of Education, 2016). 
To be able to effectively lead, principals need to see the “bigger picture”, and to 
have knowledge and insight into the complex processes taking place in their school 
(Ministry of Education, 2008). 
TIMSS collected data on principals’ perceptions of their school environment, in 
conjunction with other factors that contribute to effective schools. This chapter will 
present findings on principals’ perceptions of their school, in relation to the students’ 
science achievement.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Principals’ perceptions of climate for learning
Principals of Year 9 students were asked how they would characterise thirteen aspects of life at their school, 
listed in Exhibit 14.1. The response options were: very high; high; medium; low; and very low.
The statements the principals were most positive about were mainly related to teachers. The majority 
of principals indicated that teachers’ expectations for student achievement (90% of Year 9 students), 
teachers’ understanding of the school’s curricular goals (90% of students), and teachers’ degree of success 
in implementing the school’s curriculum (88% of students) at their school were very high or high. Other 
statements about teachers had slightly lower proportions, with the lowest being teachers’ ability to inspire 
students (74% of students).
Principals were also very positive about students’ respect for classmates who excel at school, with 87 percent 
of Year 9 students having principals who indicated this was very high or high. However, they were less 
positive about other student related aspects, like students’ ability to reach school’s academic goals (78% of 
students high or very high), and students’ desire to do well at school (73% of students high or very high). 
Compared to their responses about teachers and students, responses by the principals to parental support 
and involvement were less positive. Parental involvement in school activities, the lowest of the five parental-
related statements, had only one quarter of Year 9 students whose school principals indicated this aspect 
was very high or high. Of the five statements, more principals indicated that parental pressure for the school 
to maintain high academic standards, and parental expectations for student achievement were high or very 
high (just above two thirds of Year 9 students), compared with the other statements about parents.

As shown in Exhibit 14.3, the majority of New Zealand schools (78% of students) had high or very high 
emphasis on academic success. Compared to the international average, more New Zealand schools had 
high emphasis on academic success. Most high-achieving countries, including Hong Kong SAR (56%), 
Chinese Taipei (47%), and Japan (45%), had more schools with medium emphasis on academic success 
than New Zealand (22%). The general pattern, as observed both for the international average, and for 
New Zealand, was that the higher the academic emphasis reported by the principal, the higher the 
associated achievement scores in TIMSS for science, on average. However, in New Zealand, average 
science scores in very high and high emphasis categories were not significantly different.

Trends in principals’ perceptions of school climate and 
academic success
The questions given to principals about school climate were first introduced in TIMSS 2002/03. Comparison 
of the statements included in all three cycles since 2002/03 shows little change across the cycles on most of 
the statements. As shown in Exhibit 14.4, compared to TIMSS 2010/11 results, in 2014/15 more principals rated 
teachers’ degree of success in implementing the school’s curriculum (significant increase from 80% to 88% of 
Year 9 students), and teachers’ expectations for student achievement (significant increase from 76% to 90% 
of Year 9 students) as being very high or high in their school.

Principals’ perceptions of school discipline and student 
behaviours
As school leader, the principal plays a major role in building and sustaining an effective learning environment 
and a positive school climate. TIMSS 2014/15 asked the school principals how much of a problem a series of 
behaviours were in their school among Year 9 students (see Exhibit 14.5). Principals expressed the extent to 
which these behaviours were a problem with one of the following response options: not a problem; minor 
problem; moderate problem; or serious problem. 
The majority of Year 9 students attended schools where these behaviours were perceived by the principal to 
be a minor problem at most. Very few students (not more than 1%) had principals that acknowledged any of 
these behaviours as posing a serious problem in their school. Among the most common behaviours causing 
moderate or serious problems were arriving late at school, absenteeism, intimidation or verbal abuse among 
students, and classroom disturbance, all with more than 10 percent of Year 9 students in this category.

To summarise the extent to which school discipline and safety affects student learning, the TIMSS 
international team created the School discipline problems scale, based on principals’ views on the extent 
to which the eleven behaviours listed above occurred among lower secondary students in their school. 
To report the scale in a meaningful way, values were grouped into three categories: moderate to severe 
problems; minor problems; and hardly any problems.
Two-thirds of New Zealand Year 9 students (66%) attended schools that had minor problems with 
school discipline, as reported by principals. New Zealand principals were less positive than, on average, 
internationally (principals reported 31% of New Zealand schools having hardly any problems, compared to 
43% internationally). Most of the high-achieving countries had a proportion of lower secondary students 
similar to, or above, the international average in the hardly any problems category. This finding appears to 
be in line with student reports of bullying behaviours at school; New Zealand was one of the countries with 
relatively high proportions of Year 9 students regularly experiencing bullying behaviours (see Exhibit 12.7). 
Being in a school with less of a problem with school discipline was associated with higher achievement in 
science, both in New Zealand and, on average, internationally.

Trends in principals’ perceptions of school discipline
Even though the statements about school discipline were also asked in previous TIMSS cycles, in 2002/03 
this question was about the frequency of a series of problem behaviours occurring in the schools, and how 
severe a problem these behaviours were. In 2010/11, the question was adjusted to only ask how much of a 
problem these behaviours were in the school, with different answer options to the previous cycle. Due to 
these differences, we are able to compare the results across only TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15. According to 
the School discipline problems scale results, more New Zealand lower secondary principals reported hardly 
any problems with school discipline in 2014/15, compared with 2010/11 (Exhibit 7.2, Martin et al., 2016).
