144
CHAPTER 15:
School resources for 
Year 9 students
 Key Findings
Among the most commonly reported problems were the school building needs significant 
repair, teachers do not have adequate workspace and teachers do not have adequate support 
for using technology; more than one-quarter of Year 9 students had teachers who thought 
these were moderate or serious problems in their school.
New Zealand teachers were relatively positive about resources for teaching science, compared 
to teachers from other countries, and similar to those from some of the high-achieving 
countries. Internationally, on average, the more positive teachers were about their resources, 
the higher the average science achievement of the lower secondary students. However, in 
New Zealand, this relationship was not as strong; there was no significant difference in science 
achievement between the Year 9 students whose teachers reported moderate to severe 
problems, and those with minor problems.
Principals most commonly reported a lack of computer software/applications as affecting 
science instruction. In contrast to the responses of the Year 5 principals, teachers with a 
specialisation in science were among the least commonly reported resource shortages. A 
lack of technologically competent staff, computer technology for teaching and learning, and 
resources for students with disabilities were among the most common general resources 
reported as affecting instruction. 
Just over half of New Zealand Year 9 students were in schools where principals had indicated 
that resource shortages affected science instruction. Internationally, on average, the less 
affected the school was by a shortage of science resources, the higher the science achievement 
scores of the lower secondary students. This held true for New Zealand, but most high-
achieving countries had no difference in the achievement of lower secondary students in more 
affected and less affected schools.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

The quality and nature of the physical environment of schools and resources have 
been thought to have a significant influence on teacher attitudes, behaviours, and 
performance, which have been directly linked to student outcomes and achievement 
(Dawson & Parker, 1998; Schneider, 2002; Buckley, Schneider, & Shang, 2004). 
Roberts (1999) found that achievement in science was better when students had 
instruction using a variety of hands-on activities. 
In this chapter, we explore the physical environment of our schools and resources 
to gain an understanding of how the teaching environment and resource shortages 
potentially impact teaching and science achievement in TIMSS. Information on these 
has been obtained from the contextual questionnaires filled in by teachers and 
principals in participating schools. 
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents

Quality and availability of school buildings and resources, 
according to science teachers
Teachers in TIMSS 2014/15 were asked to describe the extent to which various issues created problems 
for them, as listed in Exhibit 15.1. They were given four response options: not a problem; minor problem; 
moderate problem; and serious problem.
The least problematic of these issues, in the opinions of the teachers, were the school classrooms are not 
cleaned often enough, with 71 percent of Year 9 students who had science teachers report that this was not 
a problem. For all the other issues, more than half of the Year 9 students had science teachers who rated 
these as at least a minor problem in their school. Among the most commonly reported problems were the 
school building needs significant repair, teachers do not have adequate workspace and teachers do not have 
adequate support for using technology; more than one-quarter of Year 9 students had science teachers who 
thought these were moderate or serious problems in their school.

The international researchers combined the responses to the statements in the Problems with school

As Exhibit 15.2 shows, New Zealand Year 9 science teachers were relatively positive about their working 
conditions, with a small proportion reporting moderate to severe problems with school conditions and 
resources, compared with teachers from other countries. High-achieving countries Chinese Taipei, Hong 
Kong SAR, and the Russian Federation had similar proportions of lower secondary students at each level of 
the scale. 
According to the international average, the more positive teachers were on the scale (that is the less likely 
they were to report on any problem), the higher the average science achievement of the lower secondary 
students (see Exhibit 15.2). However, in New Zealand, this relationship was not as strong; there was no 
significant difference in science achievement between those Year 9 students whose teachers reported 
moderate to severe problems, and those with minor problems.

Impact of shortages of resources, according to principals
Principals were asked to rate if their school’s capacity to provide instruction was affected by a shortage or 
inadequacy of general and science resources, as listed in Exhibit 15.3. This question was asked differently 
from the question asked of teachers, with the answer options being: a lot; some; a little; or not at all. Of the 
resources listed, the one most commonly seen as having an impact on science instruction (a lot, some and 
a little combined) was a lack of computer software/applications. The least commonly reported resources 
that affected science instruction were teachers with a specialisation in science, and calculators for science 
instruction. This was different from TIMSS Year 5 results, where a shortage of teachers with a specialisation in 
science was a more common factor impacting science instruction, as reported by principals. 
Of the general resources, a lack of technologically competent staff, computer technology for teaching and 
learning, and resources for students with disabilities were among the most common resources indicated as 
hindering instruction. A lack of supplies, instructional materials, and heating/cooling and lighting systems 
were less commonly reported as hindering instruction, compared to the other resources listed.

The international researchers combined principals’ responses to the statements in Exhibit 15.3 into the 
Instruction affected by science resource shortages scale. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values were 
grouped into three categories: not affected; affected; and affected a lot. As Exhibit 15.4 shows, 45 percent 
of New Zealand Year 9 students were in schools where principals had indicated that resource shortages had 
not affected science instruction, and the rest were in schools where instruction was affected by shortages. 
The principals’ responses, overall, to instruction being affected by science resource shortages in New Zealand 
schools hasn’t changed significantly from 2010/11 (Exhibit 5.7, Martin et al., 2016). 
Internationally, on average, fewer lower secondary students (27%) were in schools where principals felt that 
instruction was not affected by science resource shortages. Chinese Taipei had a smaller proportion of lower 
secondary students, compared to New Zealand, where principals felt that instruction was not affected by 
science resource shortages. On the other hand, Singapore had as high as 74 percent of lower secondary 
students not affected by resource shortages. However, Singapore, compared to other high-achieving 
countries, also had a higher proportion of lower secondary students (8%) that were affected a lot by science 
resource shortages.

Availability of laboratories
Principals were asked if the school had a science laboratory that can be used by Year 9 students. No 
definition was given in the question of what was meant by a science laboratory. Almost all Year 9 students 
(99%) in New Zealand attended schools with a science laboratory that they could use. Internationally, on 
average, 85 percent of lower secondary students had science laboratories in their schools.

School libraries
All Year 9 students in New Zealand attended schools that had a school library. Most school libraries 
contained more than 2000 print books, and more than 10 print magazines and other periodicals. Digital 
books, magazines and other periodicals were less common compared to print ones, with most libraries 
having less than 500 digital books, and less than five digital magazines and other periodicals. The number of 
print or digital resources in school library didn’t appear to have a consistent relationship with Year 9 students’ 
science achievement.
Access to specialists
New Zealand principals were asked two questions about the availability of assistance and experts. 
Specifically, the two questions were:
»»
Do teachers usually have assistance available when students are conducting science experiments?
»»
In the last two years, has your school been involved in a science project(s) where you partnered with 
someone outside your school to help students learn specific science concepts?
Half of the New Zealand Year 9 students were in schools where principals reported that teachers usually 
have assistance when students are conducting experiments. Forty seven percent of Year 9 students were in 
schools that had been involved in a science project in the last two years where they partnered with someone 
from outside the school to help students learn specific science concepts. 
Some of the partner projects involved locally available experts in the high schools or colleges, the 
universities, scientists (including parents of children in the school), and local industries. Others included a 
science roadshow and science fairs. These activities fit with the vision set out in A Nation of Curious Minds 
(Ministry of Business, Innovation and Employment and Ministry of Education, 2014)

TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
151
Conclusion
Science achievement in TIMSS 2014/15
The average (mean) science achievement of New Zealand Year 9 students in 2014/15 was the same as that of 
the students 20 years ago, and was also the same as in 2010/11. New Zealand Year 9 students’ mean science 
achievement was significantly higher than 20 countries, but lower than the mean score of 14 countries.
Was our average achievement low compared with some other countries because the test was inappropriate 
for New Zealand students? When the TIMSS test was compared to curriculum expectations for New Zealand 
Year 9 students, there were questions considered more advanced than our curriculum. However, when the 
test was reduced to contain only those appropriate to New Zealand Year 9 students, the average student 
got half the items correct. Many New Zealand Year 9 students were working at level 4 of the curriculum by 
the end of the year, rather than level 5. Perhaps not surprisingly, those students working at higher levels had 
higher science achievement.
Strengths and weaknesses in Year 9 science achievement 
New Zealand Year 9 students showed greatest strength in biology and Earth science. Chemistry has 
remained a relative weakness for New Zealand Year 9 students since 2010/11. Even though New Zealand 
Year 9 students did best on biology and Earth science questions, these were the areas of the curriculum 
least likely to be covered by their teachers. New Zealand Year 9 teachers also felt less well prepared to teach 
topics in Earth science than in other areas.
New Zealand Year 9 students did significantly better at applying and reasoning, compared to knowing in 
science. The New Zealand Year 9 students’ achievement was similar across all science content and cognitive 
domains between 2010/11 and 2014/15. The majority of New Zealand Year 9 students had teachers who 
expressed high or very high confidence in their own ability to teach science. Of the statements listed, the 
most confidence was expressed in explaining science concepts and principles by doing science experiments, 
and the least confidence was expressed in teaching science using inquiry methods.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

Equity among Year 9 students
“Over many years, the New Zealand education system has been characterised by relatively high levels of 
inequality” (Ministry of Education, 2016). One way to examine equity is to look at the gap between the high 
and low performers. The gap between the high and low achievers (range) for Year 9 students in 2014/15 
was much wider than in 2002/03; the change has occurred at both ends of the spectrum, with the top 
higher than in 2002/03, and the bottom lower than in 2002/03. Ten percent of New Zealand Year 9 students 
were classified as advanced performers (reached the advanced benchmark), while 12 percent of students 
were classified as below low performers, who did not perform simple science tasks. Compared with other 
countries, New Zealand was around the middle when considering the proportion of advanced performers, 
as well as the proportion of lower performers. High-performing countries had at least one-fifth of their lower 
secondary students classified as advanced performers, and four percent or fewer classified as below low 
performers.
There were more Year 9 students who were lower achievers in 2014/15, compared with 2002/03. 
The proportion of Year 9 students reaching each benchmark has not changed significantly between 2010/11 
and 2014/15.
Year 9 boys and girls had the same science achievement as each other, on average, but boys had a wider 
range than girls. Year 9 girls had significantly higher science achievement than 20 years ago, while boys 
have not changed much since 1994/95. Similar proportions of girls and boys reached each of the science 
achievement benchmarks in 2014/15. More girls reached the high science benchmark, and fewer boys 
reached the low benchmark (there were more boys below low), in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11. In 2014/15, 
girls did better in biology, while boys achieved higher on physics and Earth science, with no significant 
gender difference on chemistry or the cognitive domain areas of knowing, applying, and reasoning. In 
2014/15, girls saw a significant increase in the biology, applying, and reasoning domain areas, compared 
to their counterparts in 2010/11. Boys, on the other hand, saw a significant decrease in the chemistry and 
knowing domain areas, compared to those in TIMSS 2010/11. 
Equity among ethnic groupings remains a concern. One-fifth of all Year 9 students identified themselves as 
belonging in more than one ethnic grouping; the groupings were Māori, Pasifika, Pākehā/European, Asian, 
and Other. There was a wide variation in science achievement within ethnic groupings, with advanced 
achievers and very low achievers in all ethnic groupings. Māori had lower average science achievement than 
non-Māori. Similarly, Pasifika students had lower average science achievement than non-Pasifika. There 
was evidence that this achievement gap narrows when socio-economic factors are taken into account. The 
average science achievement of Year 9 students classified as Other has decreased significantly since 2010/11.
The relationship between home life and schooling outcomes can be examined in TIMSS. Students in homes 
with many resources for learning had higher science achievement, on average, than those whose homes had 
fewer resources. One in five Year 9 students regularly spoke a language other than English at home (most 
also spoke English), however, the relationship with achievement was not clear, as the extent to which these 
students were proficient in English when they started school was not gathered.
About 60 percent of Year 9 students read for enjoyment daily. Those students that read books, e-books or 
comics for enjoyment more often had significantly higher average science scores than those who read less 
often. Overall, more Year 9 girls read for enjoyment daily than boys. Within ethnic groupings, fewer Māori and 
Pasifika students read daily than non-Māori and non-Pasifika students. 
New Zealand Year 9 students were more likely go to the movies and participate in various outdoor activities 
than other leisure pastimes. Doing leisure activities didn’t appear to have a relationship with student science 
achievement. New Zealand Year 9 students spent more time on sports as an out of school activity, compared 
to other regular activities. Overall, increased time spent on regular out of school activities was associated 
with lower mean science achievement. Tutoring in science was less common in New Zealand than in many 
other countries; only 5 percent of Year 9 students attended extra lessons to excel in class, and nine percent 
did so to keep up. 
It was clear from the TIMSS results that some schools face more barriers to helping their Year 9 students 
achieve than others. Science achievement was higher, on average, for students in schools with more affluent 
students than those in schools with more economically disadvantaged students. The difference in science 
achievement between these two groupings within New Zealand was a lot higher than the international average.
152
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Resources available at school could help offset a lack of resources at home. TIMSS asked about the level 
to which a lack of resources at school was a problem. New Zealand teachers were relatively positive about 
resources for teaching science, compared to teachers from other countries, and similar to those from some 
of the high-achieving countries. Internationally, on average, the more positive teachers were about their 
resources, the higher the average science achievement of the lower secondary school students. However, in 
New Zealand, this relationship was not as strong; there was no significant difference in science achievement 
between the students whose teachers reported moderate to severe problems, and those with minor problems. 
New Zealand had a higher proportion of Year 9 students with computers available for use during science 
lessons, compared to other countries. Computers were most commonly used to look up ideas and information.
Principals most commonly reported a lack of computer software/applications as affecting science 
instruction. In contrast to Year 5, teachers with a specialisation in science were among the least commonly 
reported resource shortages. A lack of technologically competent staff, computer technology for teaching 
and learning, and resources for students with disabilities were among the most common general resources 
reported to affect instruction. Just over half of New Zealand Year 9 students were in schools where principals 
had indicated that resource shortages affected science instruction. Internationally, on average, the less 
affected the school was by a shortage of science resources, the higher the science achievement scores. 
This held true for New Zealand, but most high-achieving countries had no difference in the achievement of 
students in more affected and less affected schools.
Student attitudes to learning science
To what extent do student attitudes relate to science achievement? More than three-quarters of New Zealand 
Year 9 students value science and like learning science. However, 45 percent of Year 9 students were 
classified as not confident in science. Compared to the international averages, New Zealand had a higher 
proportion of lower secondary students who do not value science, do not like learning science and were not 
confident in science.
Overall, students who were more positive about science had higher average (mean) science achievement 
than those who were less positive. The difference in mean science achievement between the most positive 
and least positive students was larger on the Students confident in science scale than on the Students 
like learning science and Students value science scales. There was a significant increase in how much New 
Zealand Year 9 students liked learning science and valued science in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11. However, 
their confidence in science hasn’t changed significantly since 2010/11.
New Zealand Year 9 boys were slightly more positive about science than Year 9 girls; more boys like learning 
science and were confident in science, while similar proportions of boys and girls value science. This result 
was similar for Pākehā/European Year 9 boys, compared to Pākehā/European Year 9 girls. Māori Year 9 
girls and boys had similar proportions across all three scales. The difference in science achievement, on 
average, between boys who were positive about science and those who were negative was greater than the 
difference between the girls who were positive and the girls who were negative.
The Māori ethnic grouping had more Year 9 students who do not value science, do not like learning 
science and were not confident in science, compared with non-Māori students. The Pākehā/European 
ethnic grouping had more students who were confident in science, but also more students who do not 
value science, compared with non-Pākehā/European students. The Asian grouping stood out with bigger 
proportions of students who value and like learning science, compared with non-Asian students. There was no 
disproportionate representation of the Pasifika group on the Students like learning science and Students value 
science scales, but more Pasifika students were not confident in science, compared with non-Pasifika students.
Most Year 9 students had high educational expectations, with nearly three-quarters expecting to do 
some form of tertiary study. Generally, students with lower educational expectations had lower science 
achievement, on average. More girls had higher expectations than boys. Compared with non-Māori, fewer 
Māori students planned to pursue a university education. More Asian students, compared with non-Asian 
students, expected a university education. Compared to previous cycles of TIMSS, in 2014/15 more Year 9 
students expected to complete NCEA Level 2 or 3, get a national diploma at a tertiary institution, or gain 
post-graduate diplomas and degrees.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
153
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»

Teachers and teaching science
Blömeke, Olsen, & Suhl (2016), in their recent research paper using TIMSS 2010/11 results, found that teacher 
qualifications and measures of instructional quality were both significantly related to student outcomes. 
More New Zealand Year 9 students had science teachers with postgraduate qualifications in 2014/15, 
compared to 2010/11 (68%, compared to 51%). Nearly all New Zealand Year 9 students had science teachers 
who had specialised in science and/or science education (93%). On average across countries, the same 
proportion of lower secondary students had teachers with a specialisation in science and/or science 
education. Compared to the international average, slightly more New Zealand Year 9 teachers participated in 
science related professional development activities, but fewer New Zealand science teachers had had more 
than 35 hours of professional development in the past two years, compared to their peers internationally. 
The majority of New Zealand Year 9 students were positive about how their teacher engaged with them in 
their science classes. However, fewer Year 9 students found their science lessons very engaging, compared 
to the international average. Both in New Zealand and internationally, the more engaged the students were in 
their science lessons, the higher the achievement.
According to their teachers, New Zealand Year 9 students spent about 3.5 hours on science topics per 
week, which was more than the international average. New Zealand Year 9 teachers emphasised science 
investigation less often than many other countries. Overall, there was no change in the emphasis on science 
investigation for Year 9 students in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11. Emphasis on science investigation 
appeared to have no relationship with students’ science achievement in New Zealand. Almost all high-
achieving countries had a similar pattern to New Zealand. However, overall internationally, lower secondary 
students who were in classes with more frequent emphasis on science investigation had higher achievement 
than those with less frequent emphasis.
More New Zealand teachers placed major emphasis on classroom tests for monitoring Year 9 students’ 
progress. On average internationally, teachers also placed major emphasis on the assessment of students’ 
ongoing work. 
School climate for learning
A positive school climate has been shown to be associated with academic achievement, as well as the 
behavioural and social-emotional wellbeing of students (see, for example, Voight, Austin, & Hanson, 2013). 
The TIMSS results for New Zealand confirm this finding when the climate is examined from the point of view 
of students, teachers, and principals. 
Seven out of ten New Zealand Year 9 students were positive about their school, teachers and classmates, 
and nearly half had a high sense of school belonging. Students who had a high sense of belonging had higher 
science achievement than those with less of a sense of belonging. There were more students in this latest 
cycle, compared with 2010/11, agreeing that they felt safe at school, liked being in school, and thought that 
students at their school care about each other.
New Zealand Year 9 students experienced bullying behaviours at school more frequently than lower 
secondary students in many other participating countries. Compared to 2010/11, more students experienced 
the individual bullying behaviours. More boys reported experiencing bullying behaviours than girls. More 
Pākehā/European students reported experiencing bullying behaviours than non-Pākehā/European students. 
There was no difference in the proportions of Year 9 students experiencing bullying behaviours between 
Māori and non-Māori, between Pasifika and non-Pasifika, or between Other and non-Other students. 
Fewer Asian students reported experiencing bullying behaviours than non-Asian students. More students 
experienced bullying behaviours in schools with lower socio-economic status. Those students experiencing 
bullying behaviours more often had lower science achievement, on average, than those experiencing them 
less often. 
So what did the teachers think about the climate for learning? Compared to 2010/11, more science teachers 
characterised students’ desire to do well at school as high in their school in 2014/15. According to science 
teachers, more than half of the New Zealand Year 9 students were in schools with high or very high emphasis 
on academic success; this proportion was slightly higher than, on average, internationally. Higher school 
emphasis on academic success was associated with higher science scores, both in New Zealand and 
internationally.
154
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Forty percent of the Year 9 students were taught science by teachers who were categorised as very satisfied 
with their job; however, 93 percent of teachers were proud of the work they did. The proportion of Year 9 
students taught science by very satisfied teachers was lower in New Zealand than, on average, internationally. 
The proportion of New Zealand Year 9 students who had science teachers who felt their school was very safe 
and orderly was higher in New Zealand than, on average, internationally. Higher science achievement was 
associated with higher school safety.
Students who studied in classes where teachers felt that student needs were very limiting or somewhat 
limiting their science teaching had lower science achievement than those who studied in classes where 
teachers felt that student needs did not limit teaching at all. More New Zealand teachers, compared with the 
international average, reported that student needs did not limit their science teaching.
Overall, the principals were very positive about their teachers’ role in the school’s academic success. 
They were, however, less positive about parents’ support and involvement than about their teachers and 
students. The principals and science teachers had similar views on the aspects of the school’s academic 
success that involved teachers. However, a significant difference was observed in those aspects that 
involved student efforts and parental involvement; the principals were more positive than the science 
teachers about these aspects.
Overall, principals’ perceptions of their school’s academic climate don’t appear to have changed much 
since 2002/03. However, a significant increase was observed in principals’ perceptions of teachers’ degree 
of success in implementing the school’s curriculum, and teachers’ expectations for student achievement. 
More New Zealand schools reported high or very high emphasis on academic success, compared to 
the international average, and to most high-achieving countries. New Zealand Year 9 students studying 
in schools with higher emphasis on academic success, according to the principals, had higher science 
achievement, on average. 
Compared to other countries, including most of the high-achieving countries, New Zealand lower secondary 
school principals were less positive about school discipline. This was in line with student reports of bullying 
behaviours at school; New Zealand had a relatively high proportion of Year 9 students regularly experiencing 
bullying behaviours. However, more New Zealand schools had hardly any problems with school discipline 
in 2014/15, compared with 2010/11. Being in a school with less of a problem with school discipline was 
associated with higher achievement in science, both in New Zealand and, on average, internationally.
Final comment
As well as providing us with a snapshot of student achievement in science in middle primary and lower 
secondary schooling, TIMSS also provides us with valuable information about how the New Zealand 
education system changes — or does not — over time, and in an international context. This allows education 
stakeholders at all levels of the education system to reflect on the different aspects examined in TIMSS, as 
part of a review of their policies and practices.

Sampling
Schools were sampled for Year 5 and Year 9 
separately but with the intent to minimise the 
overlap and therefore to minimise the burden 
on individual schools. They were sampled from 
pre-defined groups. These pre-defined groups, 
or explicit strata, were based on decile, level of 
urbanisation, and for Year 9 only, school gender. 
This methodology meant that the schools selected 
better represented the population of schools in 
New Zealand. Within each school, at the Year 5 
level, classes were sampled with equal probability, 
and all Year 5 students within each sampled class 
were selected. At the Year 9 level, 2 classes were 
sampled and all Year 9 students within each 
sampled class were selected.
Exclusions
Each country was permitted to exclude some 
students for whom the assessment was not 
appropriate or was difficult to administer. Countries 
were required to keep the amount of excluded 
students as small as possible, with a guideline 
of five percent of the ‘target’ population as the 
maximum. Any countries that exceeded this 
value are indicated in the international exhibits. 
The target population in New Zealand for middle 
primary students was Year 5 students and for lower 
secondary was Year 9 students.
Definitions and 
technical notes
School-level exclusions in New Zealand consisted of 
very small schools (less than four Year 5 students 
or less than four Year 9 students), special education 
schools, the Correspondence School, and schools 
that provide more than 50% of their instruction in 
te reo Māori. Within-school exclusions consisted of 
special education classes, special needs students, 
students with insufficient instruction in English, and 
units within schools that provide more than 50% of 
their instruction in te reo Māori.
The New Zealand exclusion rate was 4.8% for Year 
5 and 3.1% for Year 9. 
Mean, medians, modes and 
averages
There are three measures of central tendancy, 
the mean, median, and mode. The mean of a set 
of scores is the sum of the scores divided by the 
number of scores, and is also sometimes referred 
to as ‘the average’, particularly in the international 
reports. Note that for TIMSS, as with other large-
scale studies, the means for a country are adjusted 
slightly (in technical terms ‘weighted’) to reflect the 
total population of Year 5 students, rather than just 
the sample. The mode is the value that appears the 
most out of all values. The median is the middle 
value when all the values are put in order.
This section gives a brief overview of the 
technical details and definitions applicable 
to this report. For a comprehensive 
description of the technical details 
pertaining to TIMSS see Methods and 
Procedures in TIMSS 2015 (Martin, Mullis, 
& Hooper, (Eds.), 2016).
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

Scale score points
The design of TIMSS allows for a large number of 
questions to be used in science and mathematics; 
each student answers only a portion of these 
questions. TIMSS employs techniques to enable 
population estimates of achievement to be 
produced for each country, even though a sample 
of students responded to differing selections of 
questions. These techniques result in scaled scores 
that are on a scale with a mean of 500 and a 
standard deviation of 100.
TIMSS scale centrepoint
In order to make comparisons, student 
achievement scores generated in each cycle 
are placed on the same scale. The scale was 
established during the second cycle of TIMSS to 
have a mean of 500 and a standard deviation of 
100, based on the mean of country means from 
1994/95. Equating is possible because a proportion 
of questions are the same in each assessment 
as for the two previous cycles. A score of 500 in 
2014/15 is the same as a score of 500 in all previous 
cycles.
In earlier cycles of TIMSS, an international mean 
was reported. However as the number of countries 
participating changed, this mean shifted so that 
it was difficult to make comparisons across years. 
Starting from TIMSS 2010/11, only the TIMSS scale 
centrepoint of 500 is reported. This is the same 
as the TIMSS scale average reported in previous 
cycles, but renamed to avoid confusion with a 
calculated mean of country means. 
Minimum group size for 
reporting achievement data
In this report, student achievement data is not 
reported where the group size is less than 50 
students or less than 10 schools. 
Percentile
The percentages of students performing below or 
above particular points on the scale can be used 
to describe the range of achievement. The lowest 
outer limit of achievement reported in ranges is 
the 5th percentile – the score at which only five 
percent of students achieved a lower score, and 
95% of students achieved a higher score. The 
highest outer limit is the 95th percentile – the score 
at which only five percent of students achieved a 
higher score, and 95% of students achieved a lower 
score. Therefore 90% of the Year 9 student scores 
lie between the 5th and 95th percentiles.
The range of achievement
In this report, the range of achievement is the 
difference between the low percentile score 
(the score at which only five percent of students 
achieved a lower score) and the high percentile 
score (the score at which only five percent of 
students achieved a higher score).
Inter-quartile range
The inter-quartile range is more stable than the 
full range and is the difference between the 
25th percentile score (the score at which 25% 
of students achieved a lower score) and 75th 
percentile score (the score at which 25% of 
students achieved a higher score).
Benchmarks
In order to describe more fully what achievement 
on the science scale means, the TIMSS international 
researchers have developed benchmarks. These 
benchmarks link student performance on the 
TIMSS science scale to performance on science 
questions and describe what students can typically 
do at set points on the science achievement 
scale. The international science benchmarks are 
four points on the science scale; the advanced 
benchmark (625), the high benchmark (550), 
the intermediate benchmark (475), and the low 
benchmark (400). The performance of students 
reaching each benchmark is described in relation to 
the types of questions they answered correctly.
Content domains
These are the content areas of science, known 
as domains, covered by the TIMSS assessment 
framework. The content of science at the lower 
secondary level is made up of four dimensions or 
domains; biology, chemistry, physics, and Earth 
science. (see chapter 3 for details).
Cognitive domains
The cognitive domains are the thinking processes 
and behaviours expected as students engage with 
the content ranging from simple knowledge recall 
to higher-order thinking skills, and include knowing, 
applying, and reasoning dimensions (see chapter 3 
for details).

Test-curriculum matching 
analysis
In order to investigate the extent to which the 
TIMSS assessment is relevant to each country’s 
curriculum, TIMSS conducts a Test-Curriculum 
Matching Analysis (TCMA). The TCMA is also 
used to investigate the impact of selecting only 
appropriate questions on a country’s performance. 
(for details see the TIMSS International Database 
guide) 
Created scales for 
contextual variables
Item response theory was applied to student 
responses to questions so that clustering was 
taken into account. For example if nearly every 
student gave a highly positive response to one item 
then it did not overly contribute to the sum. Each 
respondent was then given a score which was put 
on a scale. Cut points on that scale were defined 
and descriptions provided that detailed the kind of 
responses given in the original questions.
Ethnicity classification in 
TIMSS 2014/15
In previous cycles of TIMSS, New Zealand 
classified each student to one ethnic grouping 
using only prioritisation. In this cycle, any student 
who identified with more than one grouping was 
assigned to each of those groupings, in line with 
the Ethnicity New Zealand Standard Classification 
(Statistics New Zealand, 2005). TIMSS 2014/15 
classified the following ethnic groupings based on 
student responses: Māori, Pasifika, Asian, Pākehā/
European, and Other. If a student ticked both Māori 
and Samoan, they would be classified as both 
Māori and Pasifika. Using this methodology, ethnic 
groupings cannot be compared with each other 
as 22% of students are in more than one grouping 
(some are in as many as four groupings).
PIRLS
Stands for the Progress in International Reading 
Literacy Study and is being conducted under the 
auspices of the IEA (International Association 
for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement); 
managed internationally by Boston College; and 
managed within New Zealand by the Ministry of 
Education. For more information visit: http://www.
educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pirls
PISA 
Programme for International Student Assessment 
(PISA) is a triennial international survey which 
aims to evaluate education systems worldwide 
by testing the skills and knowledge of 15-year-old 
students. For more information visit: http://www.
educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pisa
School decile
School deciles indicate the extent the school 
draws their students from low socio-economic 
communities. It is calculated every 5 years following 
the Census of Population and Dwellings, and based 
on 5 socio-economic indicators for a community: 
»»
Percentage of households with income in the 
lowest 20% nationally.
»»
Percentage of employed parents in the lowest 
skill level occupational groups.
»»
Household crowding.
»»
Percentage of parents with no educational 
qualifications.
»»
Percentage of parents receiving income 
support benefits.
For more information visit: http://www.education.
govt.nz/school/running-a-school/resourcing/
operational-funding/school-decile-ratings/#About

where the sei are the standard errors of all the

Standard error
Because of the technical nature of TIMSS, the 
calculation of statistics such as means and 
proportions has some uncertainty due to (i) 
generalising from the sample to the total Year 9 
school population, and (ii) inferring each student’s 
proficiency from their performance on a subset 
of questions. The standard errors provide a 
measure of this uncertainty. In general, we can be 
95% confident that the true population value lies 
within an interval of 1.96 standard errors either 
side of the given statistic. This confidence interval 
is represented in graphs by the lines extending in 
either direction from the points.
In this cycle of TIMSS, a new method for calculating 
the standard errors has been used. Therefore, the 
standard errors for previous cycles have been 
recalculated using this new method and will be 
different from those reported earlier. This new 
methodology takes advantage of advances in 
computing and uses more information about 
variations in computations.
Statistically significant
In order to determine whether a difference 
between two means is actual, it is usual to 
undertake tests of significance. These tests take 
into account the means and the error associated 
with them. If a result is reported as not being 
statistically significant then, although the means 
might be slightly different, we do not have 
sufficient evidence to infer that they are different. 
All tests of statistical significance referred to in this 
report are at the 95% confidence level.
Weighting
Due to the use of sampling, weights need to be 
applied when analysing the TIMSS data. Weighting 
ensures that any information presented more 
closely reflects the total population of Year 9 
students, rather than just the sample. The TIMSS 
weighting takes into account school, class, and 
student level information, and the overall sampling 
weight is a product of the school, class, and 
student weights.
